5 Collective Communication

Lecture slides for this chapter

Purpose

Fleet stack focus for this chapter.

Why does communication between machines constrain large-scale machine learning systems?

Computation scales by adding processors; communication scales by moving data between them. These scale differently. Adding processors increases available arithmetic capacity, but useful work stalls when the required data does not arrive in time. Exchanging gradients, activations, and parameters can then consume more time than computing them. On one machine, shortening the dominant calculation often shortens the job. Across a fleet, that same improvement may simply make each processor reach its next dependency sooner and wait longer for the others. The cost of that wait includes every reserved accelerator, so a small delay repeated throughout training can consume a substantial budget without advancing the model. Faster links help when transfer capacity is the constraint, but the benefit depends on which physical paths the traffic shares. More bandwidth cannot remove a dependency. Sending less data introduces its own choices about numerical accuracy, memory use, and when the next computation can begin. An exchange must also deliver the correct result to every participant that needs it. A fast transfer that combines the wrong contributions does not preserve a valid training run. The engineering decision is therefore how to organize communication so that its cost remains compatible with the work it enables. Light-speed delays, bandwidth limits, and the energy cost of movement make that decision as physical as processor design. In C³ terms, collective communication connects distributed compute through coordinated exchanges whose correctness and exposed cost determine whether the fleet delivers useful scale.

Learning Objectives
  • Apply communication cost models to estimate collective duration and message-size crossover points
  • Match collective communication primitives and AllToAll schedules to parallelism strategies, topology, message granularity, and skew
  • Compare ring, tree, butterfly, double-tree, and hierarchical reduction algorithms using scale-dependent cost models
  • Evaluate topology-aware collective schedules under link-bandwidth, rank-placement, and shared-capacity constraints
  • Evaluate compression, sparsification, and error feedback by balancing communication savings against convergence risk
  • Design overlap strategies with bucket fusion, asynchronous operations, and layer-by-layer gradient scheduling
  • Specify matched measurements for calibrating collective cost models across algorithms, process groups, and topologies
Explore the concepts in this chapter

The volume’s concept map, opened on this chapter. Select a concept to read how it connects to ideas in other chapters. Open the full map ↗

5.1 From Parallelism to Communication Patterns

When 10,000 GPUs cooperate on one training update, the splits created by data, tensor, pipeline, and expert parallelism determine which results must cross device boundaries. Workers execute collective operations within participating groups and point-to-point transfers between pipeline stages. In the multi-tier fleet stack of The Fleet Stack, these exchanges occupy the Distributed Execution row of the Distribution band, where logical parallelism becomes physical traffic.

Every such split assumes that replicas, shards, stages, and experts can exchange gradients, activations, parameters, and tokens quickly enough for the optimizer to see one coherent training run. In data parallelism, each worker holds a full copy of the model and processes its own slice of the batch, so the copies must agree on one combined gradient before every update. Tensor, pipeline, and expert parallelism divide the model itself, so partial results must cross device boundaries within each step. Each of these exchanges is charged to the Fleet Law’s communication term \(\CommunicationTime(\DeviceCount)\), and the part of it not hidden behind computation counts against program goodput, the ratio of a step’s ideal execution time to its actual time (The iron law and the Fleet Law terms). Every way of splitting the model relocates work onto the network, and this chapter turns those exchanges into schedules whose latency, bandwidth, and topology costs can be computed.

↪ The partitioning strategies themselves, and the 3D parallelism cube that composes data, pipeline, and tensor parallelism into one configuration space, are developed in The Parallelism Design Space.

The gap between that assumption and the wire reveals an asymmetry in how computation and communication scale. Computation is local because each GPU works on its own data, so aggregate compute grows with the number of GPUs. Communication is global because keeping the fleet synchronized requires information to cross physical links with finite latency, bandwidth, interconnect topology, and energy. Gradient synchronization is the central case, because every synchronous data-parallel step repeats it on the full gradient. Scaling laws (principle 9) make that case harder over time, because the capability they reward comes from more parameters trained on more accelerators, so the gradient to be reduced and the group that must agree on it grow together.

Managing that global traffic begins by separating an operation’s result from the schedule that produces it. The Synchronization Backbone named AllReduce, AllGather, and AllToAll as the traffic the fabric must carry; here they become contracts on results. AllReduce1 combines corresponding tensor elements across participating workers and returns the reduced tensor to every worker. AllGather instead concatenates their shards, ReduceScatter combines contributions but leaves each worker with only a shard of the result, and AllToAll sends different data to different destinations. The parallel decomposition determines which contract each exchange needs; ring, tree, and hierarchical algorithms are alternative schedules for carrying that contract out.

1 AllReduce: The name comes from MPI and describes the result, not the schedule: a Reduce (combining every rank’s data at one root) followed by a Broadcast (returning the combined value to all ranks). A direct gather to the root concentrates raw input traffic there, but a tree can aggregate contributions along the way. The algorithms in section 5.6.3 distribute the reduction without a root.

For the initial gradient budget, consider a ring schedule: each worker passes tensor chunks to a neighbor, first accumulating their sums and then circulating the completed chunks. Each phase moves nearly one tensor’s worth of data per worker, so the bandwidth budget is approximately twice the gradient size divided by link bandwidth. The detailed round count follows once the transfer model is established.

Definition 5.1: Gradient synchronization

Gradient synchronization is the collective communication step in synchronous data-parallel training in which every worker contributes its locally computed gradient tensor to an aggregate reduction, then receives the same reduced result so all model replicas apply an identical update.

  1. Significance: Communicating all gradients of a 70B-parameter model in BF16 gives a 140 GB logical payload per replica per step. As a communication-only baseline, suppose every rank reduces that complete buffer. A ring across 1,000 GPUs at 50 GB/s per link has a bandwidth budget of approximately \(2 \times 140/50\) \(\approx\) 5.6 s of transfer per step. Model partitioning shrinks each rank’s share of that buffer, and only the portion of \(\CommunicationTime(\DeviceCount)\) left exposed after overlap adds to step time in the Fleet Law (equation 1.3).
  2. Distinction: Unlike a parameter server, which concentrates every worker’s gradient at a central aggregator, ring AllReduce spreads the exchange so that each worker sends \(2 \times (\DeviceCount-1)/\DeviceCount\times \CollectivePayloadSize\) bytes per step, approaching \(2\CollectivePayloadSize\) however large the cluster grows (section 5.6.1 develops the comparison).
  3. Common pitfall: A frequent misconception is that ring AllReduce makes every worker exchange data with every other worker. In ring AllReduce, each worker communicates with neighbors according to a schedule, and the per-node communication volume is approximately constant as the cluster grows. The scaling pressure comes from latency steps, topology, and the gradient volume per step, not from every worker opening a distinct stream to every other worker.

For standard synchronous data-parallel stochastic gradient descent (SGD), gradient synchronization supplies the shared gradient needed for one optimization trajectory. Replicas that apply different local gradients can diverge. To reproduce the intended global-batch update, they must also start from matching parameters and optimizer state, weight local contributions correctly, and apply the same update rule. The AllReduce primitive provides the reduction; convergence follows from the optimization assumptions, not from communication alone. Floating-point reduction order can still change low-order bits.

In general, a model with \(\Parameters\) parameters contributes \(2\Parameters\) bytes per data-parallel replica when all gradients are communicated in BF16 (2 bytes per parameter); stored weight precision alone does not determine that choice. For the 70-billion-parameter model in the definition above (a mid-sized dense model smaller than the 175B-parameter running instance of Archetype A, GPT-4/Llama-3), that complete logical gradient is 140 GB, and a ring moves nearly twice its input buffer size per rank.2 If a replica spans multiple ranks through model parallelism, each corresponding-shard group reduces its own portion rather than this full buffer.

2 Ring AllReduce: The algorithm dates to the HPC collective-communication literature; Patarasuk and Yuan analyzed bandwidth-optimal AllReduce algorithms for workstation clusters, Baidu’s Andrew Gibiansky popularized ring AllReduce for deep learning in February 2017, and Horovod plus NVIDIA NCCL helped make it a common distributed-training primitive (Patarasuk and Yuan 2009; Gibiansky 2017; Sergeev and Balso 2018; Jeaugey 2017).

Patarasuk, Pitch, and Xin Yuan. 2009. “Bandwidth Optimal All-Reduce Algorithms for Clusters of Workstations.” Journal of Parallel and Distributed Computing 69 (2): 117–24. https://doi.org/10.1016/j.jpdc.2008.09.002.
Gibiansky, A. 2017. Bringing HPC Techniques to Deep Learning. Baidu Research Technical Blog.
Sergeev, Alexander, and Mike Del Balso. 2018. “Horovod: Fast and Easy Distributed Deep Learning in TensorFlow.” CoRR abs/1802.05799.
Jeaugey, Sylvain. 2017. NCCL 2.0. GPU Technology Conference presentation.

Because every bandwidth term in this chapter scales linearly with payload size, the worked derivations use this compact 70B gradient (or the chapter’s reference gradient buffer first priced in the energy estimate of section 5.2) and vary rank count to isolate how each schedule scales, before section 5.7.1 prices the full 175B Archetype A model on this book’s reference cluster.

At large scale (hundreds of billions of parameters across thousands of GPUs), gradient synchronization can dominate training step time unless collective communication is systematically optimized. Understanding how to manage that overhead requires modeling data movement as physics rather than as an abstract API call.

Exposed gradient synchronization can dominate step time at scale.

Self-Check: Question

  1. A dense-model training run triples its GPU count from 64 to 192 while holding the global work per update fixed. Step time drops by only 30%, and per-GPU compute utilization falls. Which scaling mechanism should the team investigate, without treating these aggregates as proof of the cause?

    1. Backpropagation stops functioning correctly across multiple nodes because gradients cannot be computed concurrently across independent data batches.
    2. Floating-point accumulation during gradient reduction becomes numerically unstable when more than 64 GPUs participate in the collective.
    3. Host CPU memory bandwidth saturates because all optimizer states must be shuffled through host DRAM on every multi-node step.
    4. Local compute can shrink while global synchronization and shared-link transfers remain exposed; rank-aligned traces are needed to determine whether communication actually limits the step.

  2. A logical Ring AllReduce cost model uses a 140 GB input per rank, corresponding to the model-wide BF16 gradient size of a 70B-parameter model. Holding that input fixed, a team expects per-rank sent volume to grow over 100 times when the group grows from 8 to 1,000 ranks. Explain why the expectation is false and why time can still increase. Treat this as transfer arithmetic, not a claim that a full replicated training state fits on one GPU.

  3. True or False: In synchronous data-parallel distributed training, using Ring AllReduce causes each GPU network bandwidth requirement to scale quadratically with the number of GPUs \(N\) because every worker must open a direct communication socket to every other worker in the cluster.

See Answers →

5.2 The Physics of Data Movement

Whenever a gradient shard leaves an accelerator, three hardware limits bound its trip before any collective algorithm runs. The speed of light sets a propagation floor3 of roughly 5 \(\MicroUnitPrefix\text{s}\) per kilometer in fiber. High-frequency signal loss limits how far copper traces can reach before the link must convert to optics (the bandwidth-distance product of Reach and medium for copper and optics). Host-kernel networking stacks add tens of microseconds of software copy overhead, unless kernel-bypass transports such as remote direct memory access (RDMA) and GPUDirect RDMA strip per-message initiation down to a few microseconds. Collective communication also pays an energy price for every bit moved across those links, which the schedule must respect as firmly as latency and bandwidth.

3 The speed-of-light constraint: Light travels through optical fiber at approximately 200,000 km/s, or 200 meters per microsecond. For a rack-to-rack fiber path of 100 meters, propagation alone contributes approximately 500 ns in one direction. Faster switches can reduce other latency components, but reducing this propagation delay requires a shorter path or a different propagation medium.

↩︎ Kernel-bypass RDMA, which strips the per-message software overhead, and GPUDirect RDMA, which removes the host-memory bounce, are established in RDMA and GPUDirect.

Moving data consumes energy across storage arrays, interfaces, serializers, transceivers, and switches. A transfer that starts in local SRAM can stay on-package in high-bandwidth memory (HBM), cross to an intra-node peer GPU, or traverse intermediate switches to a remote node. Each step widens the physical accounting boundary that an energy estimate must include (figure 1). Each boundary activates distinct physical components: on-package links, intra-node crossbars, serializers, optical transceivers, and switch fabrics. Distance alone does not determine energy per bit, and an endpoint-only estimate cannot be compared directly with a complete fabric-path estimate. Locality remains useful because it can avoid transfers and active interfaces; the energy saving must be evaluated under the same workload, utilization, and accounting boundary.

The chapter’s reference gradient buffer of 1 GB stands for one large fused reduction, far smaller than a full model gradient but large enough that per-byte transfer, not per-message startup, governs its cost; later examples return to it by name. Reducing it around an illustrative 10,000-rank ring sends approximately twice the payload from every rank. At a fabric-path transfer cost of 35 pJ/bit, a representative midpoint of the range typical of switched fabrics rather than a measurement of any particular one, this gives about 5.6 kJ per reduction, paid again at every synchronous step. The usable rule is that communication energy is the bytes an algorithm sends multiplied by the energy per bit of every boundary those bytes cross. An algorithm that moves fewer bytes, or a placement that keeps more of them inside the node, therefore saves energy as directly as it saves time. The share of the step’s total energy that communication represents must be measured at the same accounting boundary as the compute it accompanies. The fabric-path figure charges the serializers, transceivers, and switches between two endpoints. A whole-system estimate that also charges endpoint memory reads, host software, and idle network power assigns each byte crossing the network a much larger cost, so two per-byte figures can be compared only after their boundaries match.

↪ The energy cost of moving a byte across memory, storage, and network boundaries, and why movement can outweigh arithmetic, is developed in Energy per byte.

4 Zero-copy communication: GPUDirect RDMA lets the NIC read gradients directly from accelerator memory, so a 140 GB gradient never passes through host DRAM, whereas a staged path writes and then rereads it there (280 GB of host-memory traffic per send).

These constraints govern different parts of a communication budget. Propagation latency sets a floor for every message regardless of size. Link bandwidth caps throughput once a transfer is large enough to fill the pipe. Protocol overhead adds a fixed per-message cost that penalizes fine-grained exchanges unless a kernel-bypass transport removes it. RDMA’s zero-copy path4 avoids staging each transfer through host memory and the kernel’s socket buffers, keeping that per-message cost low. Because the physical fabric fixes these limits, the collective schedule determines how many times the job pays them.

Across this section, the \(\MessageStartupLatency\)-\(\LinkBandwidth\) cost model, the LogP processor-overhead extension, and the bisection-bandwidth ceiling have priced single transfers in isolation and exposed the 5,512.7 ms physical floor for a 140 GB gradient synchronization. Which collective operations a training step actually invokes, and how often they hit that latency-or-bandwidth wall, depends on how the workload partitions its parameters, activations, and tokens across devices.

Self-Check: Question

  1. On the same fabric, one collective exchanges thousands of 4 KB messages per step while another moves a single 1 GB fused buffer. Which term governs each cost, and which lever addresses it?

    1. Both are bandwidth-bound, so a faster link shortens both by the same factor.
    2. The small messages pay per-message latency and protocol overhead on every send, so fusing messages or using a kernel-bypass transport helps; the 1 GB buffer is governed by per-byte transfer, so moving fewer bytes or adding bandwidth helps.
    3. The small messages are bandwidth-bound because there are many of them, while the 1 GB buffer is latency-bound because a large transfer takes the longest path.
    4. Both are latency-bound, because the speed of light sets a floor on every message regardless of its size.

  2. A 10,000-rank ring AllReduce reduces a 1 GB gradient buffer over a fabric path that costs about 35 pJ per bit. Set up the energy of one reduction and explain what the result implies for algorithm and placement choices.

  3. True or False: Energy per transferred bit grows in proportion to physical distance, so a transfer that travels twice as far costs about twice the energy.

See Answers →

5.3 Parallelism Strategies and Their Traffic

Once a training job splits its batch, layers, or parameters across devices, no rank holds all the state needed to finish a step on its own. A synchronous data-parallel worker computes gradients from its local batch slice, but its optimizer cannot update the weights until every replica’s gradients have been combined. Some of those reductions can start as backward layers finish, while earlier layers are still computing. Tensor ownership determines where each tensor must go, and gradient or activation readiness determines when each transfer can start.

How the model is partitioned dictates which tensors cross device boundaries and which ranks must exchange them. For Archetype A (GPT-4/Llama-3), dense gradient contributions are combined within each data-parallel group; in a hybrid layout, that group contains the matching parameter shard from each replica. AllReduce serves the replicated-gradient baseline. For Archetype B (DLRM at Scale),5 lookup requests travel to embedding-shard owners and the resulting vectors return to the workers that need them. Mixture-of-experts (MoE) routing similarly sends activations to the ranks hosting selected experts and returns their outputs. These destination-specific exchanges motivate AllToAll.

5 DLRM (Deep Learning Recommendation Model): Meta’s 2019 architecture for click-through rate prediction uses sharded embedding tables and exchanges the resulting sparse lookups across workers, an AllToAll whose limiting term section 5.8 develops.

↩︎ The three lighthouse archetypes and the C³ regimes each one stresses are established in Three systems archetypes.

At large scale, these parallelism strategies can be combined. A 3D parallelism layout combines data parallelism, tensor parallelism, and pipeline parallelism, creating distinct communication groups whose exchanges may overlap in time. In a node-local tensor-parallel example, layer reductions use NVLink, pipeline stages exchange activations and their gradients point-to-point across nodes, and data-parallel groups reduce matching parameter shards over InfiniBand. Other placements change those physical paths while retaining the logical communication requirements.

Each primitive operates on a different process group, a subset of the total GPU population that participates in that particular collective. Classical Message Passing Interface (MPI) uses a communicator as the object that names such a participating set; its communicator size is the number of ranks in that set. MPI libraries already selected collective algorithms based on communicator size and message size (Thakur et al. 2005). GPU communication libraries inherit that algorithm-selection problem and add the further challenge of coordinating overlapping process groups without creating contention between concurrent collectives.

Each parallelism strategy fixes the data it must move, the collective primitive that moves it, and the term most likely to bind (table 1), and with them which participants must exchange data. The collective primitives that follow make each exchange’s contract precise, the pricing model then charges it per round, and the collective algorithms provide its execution schedule.

In this table, expert parallelism refers to the mixture-of-experts architecture pattern, in which each token activates only a few experts. Data parallelism and fully sharded data parallel (FSDP), which divides each replica’s parameters, gradients, and optimizer state across the data-parallel ranks, generate large reductions and gathers that reward byte-efficient rings and hierarchical schedules. Tensor and pipeline parallelism, by contrast, generate smaller, more frequent messages directly on the critical path, so low software overhead and fast node-local links dominate their cost. Expert parallelism and sharded embedding tables, in contrast, produce destination-specific traffic whose bottleneck shifts among message granularity, endpoint injection and reception, fabric bisection, oversubscription, routing, and skew.

The table’s rows describe data center parallelism, so they leave out the federated Archetype C. Across all three lighthouse archetypes, the dominant exchange and its bottleneck differ.

Lighthouse 5.1: Communication archetype patterns
A gradient’s communication pattern depends on the system’s objective function and constraint regime. Each lighthouse archetype faces a distinct communication challenge, and the techniques developed in this chapter map to those challenges differently, as table 2 summarizes.

The table separates traffic pattern from limiting resource. A dense reduction, a destination-specific exchange, and an intermittent device update require different interventions even though all three serve a training workload. Hierarchical AllReduce, AllToAll schedule selection, and compression with error feedback each receive their own treatment later in the chapter.

A traffic map names which pattern a workload generates, but it does not specify what each rank must hold when the call completes. Before any of those interventions can be priced, each exchange needs a precise contract that states which ranks contribute and what each holds afterward.

  1. A machine learning engineer evaluates the communication traffic induced by different model parallelism strategies. According to the chapter’s parallelism-to-collective mapping, which mapping correctly pairs a parallelism strategy with its primary collective primitive and primary physical constraint?

    1. Fully Sharded Data Parallel (FSDP) -> Broadcast primitive -> Memory capacity constraint.
    2. Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) Routing -> AllToAll primitive -> Latency and bisection network contention constraint.
    3. Tensor Parallelism within a node -> Point-to-Point Send/Recv -> Inter-node bisection bandwidth constraint.
    4. Data Parallelism -> Reduce primitive -> Host-to-device PCIe bandwidth constraint.

  2. On a supported hardware and transport path, the mechanism that lets a NIC read and write GPU memory without staging payloads through host DRAM is known as ____ RDMA.

5.4 Collective Operation Primitives

Sending a gradient tensor to a peer does not specify which workers must contribute or where the final result belongs. Collective operations supply that group-level contract, using a vocabulary standardized by MPI and inherited by modern ML communication libraries (Message Passing Interface Forum 2015). The four contracts sketched at the start of the chapter differ in what each rank holds afterward (figure 2). AllReduce leaves an identical sum (\(\Glyph{\Sigma}\)) on every rank. AllGather leaves every rank with the concatenation of all shards. ReduceScatter sums across ranks but leaves each rank one reduced shard (\(\Glyph{\Sigma}_i\)). AllToAll performs a distributed matrix transpose, routing each rank’s \(\DestinationRankIndex\)-th chunk to rank \(\DestinationRankIndex\). Two further primitives complete the set and serve as building blocks for these four: Broadcast, in which rank 0 sends its data to all ranks, and Reduce, in which all ranks aggregate into rank 0.

↪ The bandwidth and latency cost terms of AllReduce, AllGather, ReduceScatter, AllToAll, and Broadcast, under ring, tree, and pairwise-exchange algorithms, are developed in Collective Operation Complexity.

Definition 5.2: Collective operation

Collective operation is a distributed communication pattern in which every rank in a process group invokes a matching operation to aggregate, broadcast, or redistribute data. The operation’s contract specifies the output at each rank; it does not require simultaneous entry and does not, by itself, impose a general-purpose barrier before or after the call (Message Passing Interface Forum 2015).

  1. Significance: Because the contract names only each rank’s result, the schedule that carries it decides how communication demand spreads as the group grows. A schedule that funnels every contribution through one endpoint makes the communication term \(\CommunicationTime(\DeviceCount)\) of the Fleet Law (equation 1.3) grow with \(\DeviceCount\), while a balanced schedule keeps each rank’s bytes nearly constant; section 5.6.1 quantifies the gap.
  2. Distinction: Unlike point-to-point communication, a collective defines one operation over a process group. Ranks may enter at different times, and some algorithms allow one rank to complete before another has entered; correctness depends on matching the operation sequence and argument contract across ranks, not on barrier semantics.
  3. Common pitfall: Treating a non-blocking collective as complete when its initiation call returns. The returned request or stream event represents work in flight. A dependent consumer must wait for completion, and a late rank can extend that dependency even though the collective is not a barrier.

The primitive choice is the first scaling decision because it fixes which ranks must coordinate and which result each must obtain. Once that contract is chosen, the algorithm and placement determine how the resulting traffic crosses the fabric.

5.4.1 The six core primitives

Calling a full AllReduce when each rank’s optimizer updates only a \(1/\DeviceCount\) parameter shard moves twice as many bytes as the step needs, because half the work goes into assembling full tensors that every rank immediately discards. Choosing the right primitive therefore means matching the contract in table 3 to the exact slice the next computation consumes. The table’s rows run from the root-centered patterns (Broadcast and Reduce), through the three in which every rank contributes to and receives part or all of one shared tensor (AllReduce, AllGather, and ReduceScatter), to AllToAll, in which every pair of ranks exchanges distinct data. That order describes routing rather than cost: ReduceScatter and AllGather each move half of AllReduce’s bytes because each is one phase of it. The cost column previews the algorithms derived in section 5.6, using \(\DeviceCount\) for the participating rank count, \(\CollectiveTensorSize\) for the full tensor, and \(\CollectivePayloadSize\) for the per-rank AllToAll input payload. For AllReduce, the full tensor has the same byte size as each rank’s input payload, the convention used in the following algorithm models. Two schedules appear there: a ring passes chunks between neighbors, whereas a simple full-message tree reduces up a binary tree and broadcasts back down it, forwarding the whole tensor at each of its \(2\log_2 \DeviceCount\) levels. For Broadcast and Reduce, the table counts startup rounds and bytes per tree edge separately. Each traverses one tree depth, so the elapsed transfer cost accumulates across its levels. The Tree AllReduce derivation combines those two traversals.

Ring achieves AllReduce’s bandwidth-optimal cost while the simple full-message Tree model trades bandwidth for logarithmic latency (Patarasuk and Yuan 2009; Thakur et al. 2005). AllReduce itself also decomposes into ReduceScatter followed by AllGather. The decomposition is operational as well as mathematical, since Ring AllReduce’s first phase is a ReduceScatter and its second phase is an AllGather. ReduceScatter performs arithmetic, so it does not invert AllGather despite reversing the direction of shard expansion. Full ZeRO-style sharding and PyTorch FSDP use the pair the same way, gathering parameter shards with AllGather before a module needs them and reducing gradients with ReduceScatter after backward so each rank retains only its shard for the optimizer step (Rajbhandari et al. 2020). Sharding parameters, gradients, and optimizer state across workers reduces persistent model-state memory roughly by \(\DeviceCount\times\), while temporary full-parameter buffers and prefetching determine peak memory. That memory saving is developed in Memory-efficient data parallelism with ZeRO and FSDP; the communication it adds is priced in section 5.4.2.

The same AllGather/ReduceScatter pair also appears in sequence parallelism, where an AllGather reconstructs the activation view needed for a local operation and a later ReduceScatter redistributes the result along the sequence dimension. A primitive’s semantics stay fixed even when the tensor dimension being sharded changes, so every schedule developed for the pair applies to both uses.

Beyond defining data-movement dependencies, collective operations carry explicit interface and numerical contracts. At the interface level, every rank in a process group must issue collectives in a compatible sequence; mismatched operation orders can deadlock or pair unrelated buffers. Operations also enforce shape and type contracts. AllReduce requires matching element counts and datatypes across all ranks, whereas variable-size exchanges require explicit per-destination counts and displacements. These invariants form the contract between the parallel decomposition and the communication runtime: the model decides which tensor slices each rank owns, and the collective contract guarantees that those slices can be recombined deterministically.

Reduction order introduces a second, numerical contract. Floating-point addition is not associative, so ring, tree, and switch-assisted reductions may accumulate the same values in different orders and produce different low-order bits (Goldberg 1991). A correct AllReduce therefore guarantees the specified reduction over all contributions, not bitwise identity across algorithms, rank counts, or hardware. Reproducibility-sensitive workloads must fix the algorithm and process-group configuration or use a reproducible accumulation scheme; ordinary training should treat small order-dependent differences as numerical behavior while still checking for larger rank disagreement.

Goldberg, David. 1991. “What Every Computer Scientist Should Know about Floating-Point Arithmetic.” ACM Computing Surveys 23 (1): 5–48. https://doi.org/10.1145/103162.103163.

AllToAll scales differently from AllReduce with cluster size, and section 5.8 develops the bound that governs it. With the six contracts defined, every row of table 1 now names a primitive whose output and traffic pattern are precise. The sharded data-parallel row repays closer study, because sharding changes how often those primitives run rather than which ones run.

5.4.2 FSDP communication patterns

Sharding every parameter, gradient, and optimizer-state tensor across \(\DeviceCount\) data-parallel ranks saves memory, but it replaces one end-of-step reduction with a stream of collective calls inside the forward and backward passes. Under fully sharded data parallelism (Rajbhandari et al. 2020), each rank holds only \(1/\DeviceCount\) of a layer’s weights. It must call AllGather before the layer’s forward pass to assemble the full weights. After the backward pass, it calls ReduceScatter to leave each rank the gradient for its own shard, and then frees the temporary full weights.

Rajbhandari, Samyam, Jeff Rasley, Olatunji Ruwase, and Yuxiong He. 2020. “ZeRO: Memory Optimizations Toward Training Trillion Parameter Models.” SC20: International Conference for High Performance Computing, Networking, Storage and Analysis, 1–16. https://doi.org/10.1109/sc41405.2020.00024.

Trading resident memory for on-demand assembly multiplies the collective call count. With one communication group per layer, a schedule that holds each layer’s gathered weights until its backward pass finishes issues \(2\LayerCount\) collective calls per step (one forward AllGather and one backward ReduceScatter per layer) where unbucketed data parallelism issues one AllReduce. To save peak activation memory, full-resharding FSDP instead frees the full weights right after each layer’s forward pass and runs a second AllGather before that layer’s backward pass, raising the count to about \(3\LayerCount\) calls per step. The byte volume changes far less. Because each AllGather and ReduceScatter over a large group moves nearly \(\CollectiveTensorSize\) bytes per rank across all layers combined, where \(\CollectiveTensorSize\) is the model-sized payload without optimizer state, the \(2\LayerCount\)-call schedule sends roughly the AllReduce’s \(2\CollectiveTensorSize\) per rank and the resharded \(3\LayerCount\)-call schedule sends about \(3\CollectiveTensorSize\) (\(1.5\times\)) when parameters and gradients travel at the same width. Different parameter and reduction datatypes shift that ratio. The call count carries its own price, because every call pays a startup cost. An effective startup cost \(\CollectiveCallStartupLatency\) contributes approximately \(2\LayerCount\CollectiveCallStartupLatency\) per step before overlap, or \(3\LayerCount\CollectiveCallStartupLatency\) with resharding. FSDP is therefore more sensitive to \(\CollectiveCallStartupLatency\) than an unbucketed data-parallel reduction even though its bytes barely change. The counts describe one grouping policy, since FSDP units need not coincide with layers and bucketed data parallelism also splits its reduction to enable overlap. FSDP implementations mitigate the startup cost through prefetching and communication-stream pipelining, but the relevant \(\CollectiveCallStartupLatency\) must come from a matched collective benchmark rather than a wire-latency specification.

↪ The ZeRO stages and the reshard-after-forward FSDP schedule, with the memory each saves and the communication each adds, are developed in Memory-efficient data parallelism with ZeRO and FSDP.

Selecting the right collective algorithm at each invocation requires distinguishing transfer cost from dependency exposure. An FSDP AllGather is latency-sensitive in the scheduling sense, because a layer cannot compute until its full parameters arrive. Its transfer can nevertheless be bandwidth-bound when the parameter tensor is large. Prefetching can hide part of that gather, while a ReduceScatter can overlap with the remaining backward computation. Neither dependency alone selects Ring or Tree; message size, group size, topology, and the available overlap window jointly determine the useful schedule.

The primitives specify what must be communicated, and sharding shows how often a training step invokes them. Choosing a schedule for each invocation first requires a price for every round of an exchange.

Self-Check: Question

  1. How does the communication pattern of Fully Sharded Data Parallel (FSDP / ZeRO-3) differ structurally and operationally from standard Distributed Data Parallelism (DDP)?

    1. DDP communicates twice per transformer layer using Point-to-Point Send/Recv, whereas FSDP communicates only once at the end of the step using Broadcast.
    2. FSDP replaces all network communication with CPU host memory paging, eliminating collective communication entirely.
    3. DDP reduces ready gradient buckets, whereas layer-wrapped full-shard FSDP with resharding typically issues \(3N_L\) collectives: forward and backward AllGathers plus backward ReduceScatters, increasing launch-latency sensitivity.
    4. FSDP eliminates AllGather operations by permanently keeping full model parameters in every GPU high-bandwidth memory.

  2. In Fully Sharded Data Parallel (FSDP), when does a parameter AllGather or gradient ReduceScatter add exposed delay to a training step? Explain why the primitive name alone does not identify a latency-bound or bandwidth-bound transfer.

  3. The mathematical identity that an AllReduce can be decomposed into an intra-group ____ phase (where each worker retains a reduced chunk) followed by an ____ phase (where workers circulate and concatenate the reduced chunks) forms the foundational communication mechanism behind both Ring AllReduce and ZeRO-3/FSDP memory sharding.
