5.5 Pricing Collectives by Rounds and Chunks

A gradient bucket of tens of megabytes is large enough to saturate a single network link. Yet when a thousand-GPU ring divides that bucket into a thousand shards, each hop moves only tens of kilobytes and can spend more time starting the transfer than sending its bytes. Pricing a collective therefore requires separating the fixed time to initiate each message from the rate at which bytes move once in transit.

The \(\MessageStartupLatency\)-\(\LinkBandwidth\) model \(\PointToPointTransferTime(\PointToPointMessageSize) = \MessageStartupLatency+ \PointToPointMessageSize/\LinkBandwidth\) prices a transfer over a single link (principle 5), separating a fixed startup cost (\(\MessageStartupLatency\)) from a per-byte transfer cost (\(\PointToPointMessageSize/\LinkBandwidth\)) (Hockney 1994). A collective pays that price at every step of its schedule, so the two terms, multiplied by the schedule’s round count and traffic factor, determine algorithm selection across the fleet.

Hockney, Roger W. 1994. “The Communication Challenge for MPP: Intel Paragon and Meiko CS-2.” Parallel Computing 20 (3): 389–98. https://doi.org/10.1016/s0167-8191(06)80021-9.

↩︎ The \(\MessageStartupLatency\)-\(\LinkBandwidth\) model for a single link and its latency-dominated and bandwidth-dominated regimes are established in The \MessageStartupLatency-\LinkBandwidth performance model.

↪ Worked message-size regimes and crossover calculations for this model are developed in The \MessageStartupLatency-\LinkBandwidth Communication Model.

For collectives, the parameters must describe the effective path. Latency (\(\MessageStartupLatency\)) is the startup cost paid at every step of a schedule, so it includes recurring software overhead, PCIe traversal, and network switching time; one-time communicator initialization is a separate setup cost. Bandwidth (\(\LinkBandwidth\)) is the sustained per-direction transfer rate. The critical message size \(\LatencyBandwidthCrossoverSize = \MessageStartupLatency\cdot \LinkBandwidth\), where the two terms are equal, carries over unchanged. It must, however, be compared against each step’s chunk (\(\CollectivePayloadSize/\DeviceCount\) in an \(\DeviceCount\)-rank ring) rather than the whole tensor \(\CollectivePayloadSize\). That is why a large bucket can still fall below the inter-node crossovers in table 4. Below \(\LatencyBandwidthCrossoverSize\), a step is latency-bound; above it, bandwidth-bound.

The crossover explains two recurring optimizations. For InfiniBand NDR at \(\MessageStartupLatency\approx\) 1.5 μs and \(\LinkBandwidth\approx\) 50 GB/s, the knee sits at \(\LatencyBandwidthCrossoverSize \approx\) 75 KB, the value The \MessageStartupLatency-\LinkBandwidth performance model computed for a single transfer. A 4 KB routing message is far below \(\LatencyBandwidthCrossoverSize\), so the startup cost dominates. If 100 ready messages have compatible destinations and can share one transfer, fusing them into a 400 KB message reduces the modeled cost from approximately \(100\MessageStartupLatency=\) 150 μs to \(\MessageStartupLatency+ \PointToPointMessageSize/\LinkBandwidth\approx\) 9.5 μs. That is roughly a 16× improvement before packing costs. More generally, \(\SplitMessageCount\) serialized messages of size \(\PointToPointMessageSize/\SplitMessageCount\) can cost up to \(\SplitMessageCount\times\) as much as one message of size \(\PointToPointMessageSize\) when \(\PointToPointMessageSize/\SplitMessageCount\ll \LatencyBandwidthCrossoverSize\). Fusion is the first optimization, and it motivates framework gradient buckets and destination-aware token coalescing.

Compression is the second, and the crossover tells where it pays. A 140 GB gradient tensor sits far above \(\LatencyBandwidthCrossoverSize\), so shrinking its bytes shrinks the dominant \(\PointToPointMessageSize/\LinkBandwidth\) term directly, provided encoding and aggregation costs are counted. Even keeping only the largest 1 percent of values (Top-k sparsification, developed in section 5.9) leaves 1.4 GB of values before their indices are added, still far above \(\LatencyBandwidthCrossoverSize\), so the saving remains a byte saving. Compressing a 4 KB routing message, by contrast, offers little, because its unchanged \(\MessageStartupLatency\) term already dominates.

Across the typical data center interconnects tabulated in table 4, the critical-size column carries the load-bearing pattern: intra-node NVLink stays latency-bound up to several hundred kilobytes, whereas the inter-node fabrics cross over below roughly 100 KB. A message between those crossover sizes can therefore be latency-bound inside a node but bandwidth-bound once it travels between nodes.

The NVLink and NDR rows show the roughly ninefold per-direction cliff of Bandwidth hierarchy, which The Synchronization Backbone traces across a decade of interconnect generations. Even on the lower-crossover inter-node fabric, a bucket far above \(\LatencyBandwidthCrossoverSize\) can still travel in chunks below it because a ring divides the bucket among its \(\DeviceCount\) ranks. Pricing one bucket at two group sizes shows when that happens and what it costs.

Napkin Math 5.1: The critical message size
Problem: A data-parallel job reduces a 25 MB gradient bucket with Ring AllReduce over InfiniBand NDR, with \(\MessageStartupLatency=\) 1.5 μs and \(\LinkBandwidth=\) 50 GB/s. The bucket is hundreds of times larger than the single-transfer knee. Does each step of the ring run in the bandwidth-bound regime on 64 GPUs, and on the 1,024-GPU reference cluster?

Math:

  1. Knee: \(\LatencyBandwidthCrossoverSize = \MessageStartupLatency\cdot \LinkBandwidth=\) \(1.5 \times 10^{-6}\) s \(\times\) \(5 \times 10^{10}\) B/s \(=\) 75 KB.
  2. Chunk per step: A ring sends \(\CollectivePayloadSize/\DeviceCount\) per step, 390.6 KB on 64 ranks (above the knee) but only 24.4 KB on 1,024 ranks (below it).
  3. Ring time \(2(\DeviceCount-1)\left(\MessageStartupLatency+ \frac{\CollectivePayloadSize}{\DeviceCount\LinkBandwidth}\right)\): about 1.2 ms on 64 ranks, of which the byte term is 1.0 ms; about 4.1 ms on 1,024 ranks, where the byte term is still only 1.0 ms.

Systems insight: The regime belongs to the chunk, not to the tensor. The byte term barely moves as the group grows, but at 1,024 ranks each step carries a chunk below \(\LatencyBandwidthCrossoverSize\), so startup dominates and the same bucket takes several times longer. Large language model (LLM) gradients are bandwidth-bound as tensors, yet a flat ring over a large group can still carry them in latency-bound steps. The levers then shift to larger buckets, fewer rounds, and hierarchy, which is why the algorithms of section 5.6 compete on round count as well as bytes.

For collectives, the two regimes map onto different levers. In the latency-bound regime \((\PointToPointMessageSize< \LatencyBandwidthCrossoverSize)\), where MoE routing tokens, scalar reductions, and the small chunks of a large-group schedule sit, the levers are fusion, fewer schedule rounds, fewer hops, and a leaner software stack. In the bandwidth-bound regime \((\PointToPointMessageSize\gg \LatencyBandwidthCrossoverSize)\), where LLM gradients and optimizer states sit, they are compression, a byte-efficient algorithm, and link aggregation across multiple network interface cards (NICs). Classifying each exchange, and each chunk within it, into one regime is the diagnostic skill the rest of the chapter applies.

Checkpoint 5.1: Per-chunk pricing diagnostics

Verify your understanding of per-chunk pricing regimes:

The pricing model charges a schedule per round and per chunk, but it does not choose the schedule. AllReduce, the most performance-critical primitive, admits several schedules whose round counts and byte volumes differ sharply, and for a large gradient the choice can decide whether gradient synchronization can be hidden at all.

Self-Check: Question

  1. A single-transfer model uses effective startup cost \(\alpha=1.5\,\mu\text{s}\) and per-direction bandwidth \(\beta=50\text{ GB/s}\). Which conclusion follows for a 4 KB transfer, and what must be added before classifying a complete gradient AllReduce?

    1. The single-transfer knee is 75 KB, so startup dominates the 4 KB transfer. A complete AllReduce additionally requires the schedule’s round count and chunk size, then calibration for its placement.
    2. The 4 KB transfer is bandwidth-bound because adding ranks makes every individual transfer saturate its port.
    3. The 75 KB knee is a maximum buffer size, so larger messages cannot be sent over this link.
    4. The critical message size defines the maximum possible buffer size that can be transferred in a single CUDA stream without host CPU intervention.

  2. Explain why small-message collective timings can exceed a bare-wire prediction by a much larger relative factor than large-message timings. Which measurements are needed before using this difference in a cluster capacity plan?

See Answers →

5.6 AllReduce Algorithms and Topologies

A correct AllReduce must give each of 1,000 GPUs the reduced sum of all 1,000 gradient contributions, subject to the floating-point reduction contract, and the algorithm that delivers it sets the cluster’s scaling ceiling. The naive answer, in which every GPU sends to one central server whose single network link must carry every worker’s gradient, fails first, which makes it the right place to start.

5.6.1 Naive approaches vs. the bandwidth bottleneck

Consider a naive implementation using a parameter server (a star topology), with a dedicated server in addition to the workers. All \(\DeviceCount\) workers send their gradients to that server; it sums them and sends the result back. The server must receive \(\DeviceCount\times \CollectivePayloadSize\) bytes and send \(\DeviceCount\times \CollectivePayloadSize\) bytes, so its bandwidth budget grows as \(\CommunicationTime \propto \DeviceCount\times \CollectivePayloadSize/ \LinkBandwidth\). At 1,000 GPUs, concentrating those transfers at one server creates a scaling bottleneck even if the arithmetic is fast.

This naive approach captures the pressure that early distributed ML frameworks had to manage. Parameter-server systems receive, aggregate, and redistribute model updates through a server tier, and practical implementations shard the parameter space to spread that load (Dean et al. 2012; Li et al. 2014). Sharding reduces the per-server traffic from \(\DeviceCount\times \CollectivePayloadSize\) to \(\DeviceCount\times \CollectivePayloadSize/\ParameterServerCount\) (where \(\ParameterServerCount\) is the number of servers). The server tier still grows as workers and model state grow, and it introduces additional complexity in parameter partitioning and consistency management.

Dean, Jeffrey, Greg Corrado, Rajat Monga, Kai Chen, Matthieu Devin, Quoc V. Le, Mark Z. Mao, et al. 2012. “Large Scale Distributed Deep Networks.” In Advances in Neural Information Processing Systems (NeurIPS), edited by Peter L. Bartlett, Fernando C. N. Pereira, Christopher J. C. Burges, Léon Bottou, and Kilian Q. Weinberger, vol. 25. Curran Associates.
Li, M., D. G. Andersen, J. W. Park, A. J. Smola, A. Ahmed, V. Josifovski, J. Long, E. J. Shekita, and B.-Y. Su. 2014. “Scaling Distributed Machine Learning with the Parameter Server.” Proceedings of the 2014 International Conference on Big Data Science and Computing, 583–98. https://doi.org/10.1145/2640087.2644155.

The bottleneck comes from concentration, not from total traffic. Every AllReduce algorithm moves more total data as workers are added, because every worker’s contribution must be combined and every worker must receive the result. A star, however, routes all of that traffic through one endpoint, so the server’s time grows as \(\DeviceCount\times \CollectivePayloadSize/\LinkBandwidth\). A ring spreads the same work so that each rank sends about \(2\CollectivePayloadSize\) and its time stays near \(2\CollectivePayloadSize/\LinkBandwidth\) however large \(\DeviceCount\) becomes. The ratio of the two is about \(\DeviceCount/2\), so at 1,024 workers the server’s byte time is roughly 500× the ring’s, before startup, arithmetic, or contention are counted. Bandwidth optimality makes that comparison precise by measuring an algorithm’s required byte movement against a lower bound under a stated communication model, which the next derivation supplies for AllReduce.

War Story 5.1: When the parameter server became the bottleneck (2017)
Context: In 2017, Alexander Sergeev and Mike Del Balso’s team at Uber open-sourced Horovod as part of Uber’s Michelangelo ML platform, where TensorFlow training jobs needed to scale across many GPUs without forcing every model author to rewrite large portions of training code (Sergeev and Balso 2018).

Mechanism: TensorFlow’s stock distributed training relied on a parameter-server pattern that concentrated gradient traffic through a central tier. As worker count \(\DeviceCount\) grew, the central server link saturated, degrading scaling efficiency.

Impact: Scaling training beyond a single node resulted in severe throughput bottlenecks and required complex, model-specific parameter partitioning.

Fix: Uber adopted Baidu’s ring AllReduce algorithm (Gibiansky 2017), wrapping bandwidth-optimal collective reduction behind a lightweight API that replaced parameter servers with ring communication.

Systems lesson: A collective algorithm is also a developer interface. Scaling improves when the communication primitive is both bandwidth efficient and easy enough that teams actually use it correctly.

5.6.2 The bandwidth-optimal lower bound

Before choosing a schedule to replace the parameter server, we need to know the minimum byte volume any point-to-point AllReduce must move when \(\DeviceCount\) ranks each start with an \(\CollectivePayloadSize\)-byte gradient and neither compression nor in-network switch reduction is used. Reducing one independent data item across \(\DeviceCount\) ranks and distributing its result requires at least \(2(\DeviceCount-1)\) item transfers in aggregate (Patarasuk and Yuan 2009). The count has two halves. Combining \(\DeviceCount\) contributions that start on \(\DeviceCount\) different ranks takes at least \(\DeviceCount-1\) transfers, because each transfer combines at most two partial sums and so reduces the number of separate partial sums by at most one. Delivering the finished value to the other \(\DeviceCount-1\) ranks then takes at least one transfer each. For an \(\CollectivePayloadSize\)-byte tensor, the minimum aggregate sent volume is therefore \(2(\DeviceCount-1)\CollectivePayloadSize\) bytes. At least one rank must send the average share, \(2(\DeviceCount-1)\CollectivePayloadSize/\DeviceCount\) bytes; a balanced algorithm assigns that share to every rank.

Take \(\LinkBandwidth\) as each rank’s available send bandwidth, with equal receive bandwidth that can operate simultaneously. Dividing the required sent volume by \(\LinkBandwidth\) gives the full-duplex bandwidth lower bound:

\[\AllReduceMinimumBandwidthTime= \frac{2(\DeviceCount-1)}{\DeviceCount} \cdot \frac{\CollectivePayloadSize}{\LinkBandwidth}\]

As \(\DeviceCount\) grows large, the bandwidth lower bound approaches \(2\CollectivePayloadSize/\LinkBandwidth\), independent of \(\DeviceCount\) (principle 11). Ring AllReduce matches the required byte volume under this model; startup and local reduction still add time (Patarasuk and Yuan 2009; Thakur et al. 2005). On a 64-GPU cluster synchronizing the chapter’s 1 GB reference buffer over 50 GB/s links, the ring’s byte term is about 39.4 ms, establishing the physical floor against which every concrete AllReduce schedule that follows must be judged.

5.6.3 Ring AllReduce

To hit that \(2(\DeviceCount-1)\CollectivePayloadSize/\DeviceCount\) byte floor without funneling traffic through a central server, Ring AllReduce arranges nodes in a logical loop (\(0 \to 1 \to \dots \to \DeviceCount-1 \to 0\)) where each node sends to its successor while receiving from its predecessor at the same time. That even chunk schedule spreads the required byte volume across every rank and reaches the bandwidth lower bound in the ideal endpoint model (Patarasuk and Yuan 2009).

The algorithm splits the vector of size \(\CollectivePayloadSize\) into \(\DeviceCount\) chunks and then alternates communication and local reduction in two phases, a ReduceScatter phase (often called scatter-reduce) followed by an AllGather phase. Algorithm 1 states the mechanics before the figure and trace make the same dataflow concrete.

\(N\)

Across the \(2(\DeviceCount-1)\) send/receive rounds each rank moves \(\CollectivePayloadSize/\DeviceCount\) bytes per round, for \(2(\DeviceCount-1)\CollectivePayloadSize/\DeviceCount\) bytes sent and the same received. The bandwidth term therefore reaches the information-theoretic lower bound, while the latency term grows as \(2(\DeviceCount-1)\MessageStartupLatency\). On a four-GPU ring with wrap-around links, the state progresses in two phases (figure 3). In row (a), \(\DeviceCount-1 = 3\) ReduceScatter steps circulate gradient chunks A through D until each GPU holds exactly one fully reduced partition (green \(\Glyph{\Sigma} \Glyph{A}\) through \(\Glyph{\Sigma} \Glyph{D}\)). In row (b), \(\DeviceCount-1 = 3\) subsequent AllGather steps forward those finished shards around the ring until every device holds the complete sum.

Logical ring edges may share physical links, and startup, local reduction, or contention can leave transfer capacity unused, so byte optimality does not guarantee peak physical-link utilization. To see how the logical schedule itself reaches that byte lower bound, consider 4 GPUs reducing a vector of 4 elements by summation. Each GPU \(\RankIndex\) starts with a local gradient vector \(\LocalGradient_i = [\FirstRingTraceElement_i, \SecondRingTraceElement_i, \ThirdRingTraceElement_i, \FourthRingTraceElement_i]\) split into 4 chunks (one per GPU), and the algorithm proceeds through two phases.

Example 5.1: Ring AllReduce: Two-phase example (4 GPUs)
Scenario: Four GPUs (\(0 \to 1 \to 2 \to 3 \to 0\)) perform a Ring AllReduce on local vectors \([1, 5, 3, 7]\), \([2, 6, 4, 8]\), \([3, 7, 5, 9]\), and \([4, 8, 6, 10]\) to compute the element-wise sum \([10, 26, 18, 34]\).

Diagnosis: The trace has to show three properties of the schedule, namely that it takes \(2(\DeviceCount-1) = 6\) steps, that every GPU sends and receives in every step, and that each step moves only one chunk. Ring AllReduce splits each vector into \(\DeviceCount=4\) chunks \(\FirstRingTraceElement\), \(\SecondRingTraceElement\), \(\ThirdRingTraceElement\), \(\FourthRingTraceElement\). In ReduceScatter step \(\RingStepIndex\) (\(\RingStepIndex= 1, 2, 3\)), GPU \(\RankIndex\) sends its running copy of chunk \((\RankIndex-\RingStepIndex+1) \bmod 4\) to GPU \((\RankIndex+1) \bmod 4\), which adds it to its own copy. After the third step, each GPU owns one fully reduced chunk. In AllGather step \(\RingStepIndex\), GPU \(\RankIndex\) forwards the finished chunk it completed or received most recently to GPU \((\RankIndex+1) \bmod 4\), which stores it without arithmetic. In each of the six steps, every GPU receives one chunk and updates the value it holds (table 5).

Every cell in every row is filled, so every GPU receives (and, symmetrically, sends) one chunk in every step, and after the sixth step every GPU holds [10, 26, 18, 34].

Systems lesson: Ring AllReduce achieves the optimal byte volume under the stated model: each GPU sends \(2\frac{\DeviceCount-1}{\DeviceCount}\CollectivePayloadSize\) total bytes, approaching \(2\CollectivePayloadSize\) as \(\DeviceCount\) grows, while the startup-round count continues to grow. Physical-link utilization also depends on placement and runtime overhead.

Checkpoint 5.2: Ring AllReduce mechanics

Verify your understanding of bandwidth-optimal reduction:

Generalizing the six-step trace of example 5.1 to \(\DeviceCount\) ranks yields the closed-form cost model for those checkpoint questions: each node sends and receives \(\frac{\CollectivePayloadSize}{\DeviceCount}\) bytes in each of the \(2(\DeviceCount-1)\) steps. \[ \RingAllReduceCompletionTime = \underbrace{2(\DeviceCount-1)\MessageStartupLatency}_{\text{Latency Term}} + \underbrace{2\frac{\DeviceCount-1}{\DeviceCount} \frac{\CollectivePayloadSize}{\LinkBandwidth}}_{\text{Bandwidth Term}} \]

The bandwidth term approaches \(2\CollectivePayloadSize/\LinkBandwidth\) as \(\DeviceCount\to \infty\), reaching the full-duplex lower bound: each rank sends nearly \(2\CollectivePayloadSize\) bytes across the two phases and receives the same amount concurrently. In contrast, the latency term scales linearly with \(\DeviceCount\). Across 10,000 nodes, 19,998 sequential startup rounds accumulate substantial latency, which explains why Ring AllReduce becomes uncompetitive for small payloads. For a multi-gigabyte gradient buffer at moderate rank count, however, the bandwidth term dominates completely, as the 70B calculation in notebook 5.2 confirms.

Napkin Math 5.2: AllReduce cost for a 70B model
Problem: Consider a communication-only baseline for a 70B parameter model across 64 GPUs connected by InfiniBand NDR (50 GB/s per port). The bandwidth term barely depends on rank count, so this smaller group reproduces the bandwidth-only estimate from the opening definition while keeping the latency term visible. Each rank contributes a complete logical gradient buffer communicated in BF16 (2 bytes per parameter). This calculation isolates collective cost without establishing that the training state fits on each GPU. How long does one AllReduce take?

Step 1: Size the gradient payload. Each rank in this baseline contributes the complete logical gradient tensor of \(7 \times 10^{10} \times 2\ \text{bytes}\) \(=\) 140 GB. A hybrid layout that partitions the model would instead reduce corresponding gradient shards within smaller groups and requires its own calculation.

Step 2: Apply the Ring AllReduce bandwidth formula. \[\RingAllReduceBandwidthTime= 2 \cdot \frac{\DeviceCount-1}{\DeviceCount} \cdot \frac{\CollectivePayloadSize}{\LinkBandwidth}\]

Substituting: \(\RingAllReduceBandwidthTime\) = 1.96875 \(\times\) 140 GB / 50 GB/s \(\approx\) 5,512.5 ms.

Step 3: Add the latency term. \[\RingAllReduceStartupTime= 2(\DeviceCount-1) \cdot \MessageStartupLatency\]

Substituting: \(\RingAllReduceStartupTime=\) 126 \(\times\) 1.5 μs = 0.2 ms.

Step 4: Total communication time. Total: \(\RingAllReduceCompletionTime \approx\) 5,512.5 ms + 0.2 ms \(\approx\) 5,512.7 ms.

Systems insight: The modeled full-buffer AllReduce takes over five seconds under the stated link and rank assumptions, and almost all of it is the bandwidth term. Its effect on step time depends on how much remains exposed relative to useful computation. When it is fully exposed, halving it saves about 2.8 s per step; across a 100,000-step campaign on these 64 GPUs, that is roughly 4,900 GPU-hours of reserved accelerator time. Large-scale training systems can launch reductions for output-side gradients while input-side layers are still computing. Partitioned training also changes each rank’s payload and participating group, so this complete-gradient budget must be recomputed for the actual layout.

5.6.4 Tree AllReduce

Ring AllReduce’s byte efficiency comes with many startup rounds. As the group grows, the \(2(\DeviceCount-1)\MessageStartupLatency\) term can exceed the byte-transfer term for a fixed payload; the crossover depends on the measured path parameters. Tree AllReduce reduces the number of dependent rounds. In the simple reduce phase, pairs combine contributions and forward partial sums toward a root, taking \(\log_2 \DeviceCount\) steps for a balanced power-of-two group. The broadcast phase distributes the result in another \(\log_2 \DeviceCount\) steps. This shorter dependency chain motivates the comparison before bandwidth costs are counted.

Ring latency grows with N; tree stays logarithmic.

The simple full-message tree loses parallel activity as contributions converge. A ring activates all \(\DeviceCount\) logical edges in each round. In a pairwise tree reduction, the first round has \(\DeviceCount/2\) senders and \(\DeviceCount/2\) receivers; receiving is useful work, not idleness. The next round has \(\DeviceCount/4\) senders and \(\DeviceCount/4\) receivers, and the final reduction exchange involves two ranks while the remaining \((\DeviceCount-2)\) have completed their upstream contribution. Sending the full message through successive levels adds byte-transfer time along the dependency path. This is a limitation of the illustrated schedule, not every tree-based implementation or every physical link mapping.

The resulting time complexity depends on the specific tree variant. A simple reduce-then-broadcast tree has logarithmic latency but nonuniform traffic and root/interior bottlenecks. Recursive doubling, in which partners at doubling distances exchange the entire tensor in each of \(\log_2 \DeviceCount\) rounds, has the same logarithmic round count and incurs \(\mathcal{O}(\log \DeviceCount)\) full-message bandwidth per rank. The following simplified model captures the latency advantage and the potential bandwidth penalty of tree-like collectives: \[ \TreeLikeAllReduceTime\approx \underbrace{2\log_2 \DeviceCount\cdot \MessageStartupLatency}_{\text{Latency}} + \underbrace{2 \log_2 \DeviceCount\frac{\CollectivePayloadSize}{\LinkBandwidth}}_{\text{Bandwidth penalty in full-message exchange variants}} \]

The latency term is logarithmic (\(\mathcal{O}(\log \DeviceCount)\)). For 1,024 nodes, Ring needs 2,046 steps while Tree needs only 20. That roughly 100× reduction in steps makes Tree attractive for small, frequent collectives across large process groups, such as scalar reductions, control messages, or small-batch activation reductions. The bandwidth term carries the penalty, because tree-like algorithms can underutilize links or concentrate traffic on interior links, and recursive-doubling-style full-message exchanges add a \(\log_2 \DeviceCount\) bandwidth factor. These penalties make naive tree variants a poor choice for data parallelism’s large gradient AllReduce, where bandwidth efficiency determines whether the network is saturated or partially idle.

For large-scale data parallelism, this bandwidth penalty decides the comparison whenever the implementation repeatedly exchanges full messages or creates root/interior bottlenecks. On the 64-GPU cluster of section 5.6.2, the simple tree forwards the whole tensor once per level on the way up and once per level on the way down, \(2\log_2 64 = 12\) full-message transfers. Its byte time is about 240 ms against the ring’s 39.4 ms, or 6.1× the ring’s byte time. The difference of roughly 200 ms per step would accumulate to about 5.6 hours over a 100,000-step run if fully exposed, although actual wall-clock savings also depend on startup, placement, and overlap. For a 70B-parameter model’s 140 GB gradient AllReduce across 1,000 GPUs, a bandwidth-inefficient tree-like variant can erase the latency advantage that motivated it. Optimized double-tree algorithms exist precisely to keep logarithmic latency without imposing that naive bandwidth penalty.

Small activation reductions provide a useful case for fewer startup rounds. For an eight-GPU group communicating a 1 MB tensor, the simple model reduces Ring’s \(2(8-1)\MessageStartupLatency= 14\MessageStartupLatency\) to Tree’s \(2\log_2(8)\MessageStartupLatency= 6\MessageStartupLatency\), but Tree’s byte term remains about 3.4× Ring’s. Whether the saved startups outweigh those extra byte transfers depends on the measured latency and bandwidth. Tensor-parallel collectives also have a scheduling constraint independent of this comparison: a layer that needs the reduced activation must wait for it. A critical-path dependency does not by itself imply a latency-dominated transfer or select a tree algorithm.

5.6.5 Recursive halving-doubling (butterfly)

The simple full-message Tree reduces startup rounds but repeats more byte-transfer work than Ring. Recursive halving-doubling, sometimes called the Butterfly algorithm, combines logarithmic rounds with the optimal byte volume under the flat-network model. It pairs ranks exactly as recursive doubling does, but where recursive doubling exchanges the whole tensor in every round, halving-doubling halves the exchanged region in each ReduceScatter round and doubles it in each AllGather round. That difference removes the \(\log_2 \DeviceCount\) byte penalty. For a power-of-two group, it uses \(2\log_2 \DeviceCount\) rounds: \(\log_2 \DeviceCount\) ReduceScatter rounds followed by \(\log_2 \DeviceCount\) AllGather rounds. A partner-bit index \(\PartnerBitIndex\) selects the XOR pairing with mask \(2^\PartnerBitIndex\). The ReduceScatter trace below visits those bits in increasing order as messages shrink; the matching AllGather reverses that order as the assembled result grows.

In the ReduceScatter phase (first \(\log_2 \DeviceCount\) rounds), GPU \(\RankIndex\) partners with GPU \(\RankIndex\oplus 2^\PartnerBitIndex\) (XOR of indices). Because the two partners made identical retention choices in all earlier rounds, both enter round \(\PartnerBitIndex\) holding partial sums for the exact same \(\CollectivePayloadSize/2^\PartnerBitIndex\)-byte region of the tensor. They split that active region in half, retain complementary halves of size \(\CollectivePayloadSize/2^{\PartnerBitIndex+1}\), and send the other half to each other to add into the half each retained. Across the \(\log_2 \DeviceCount\) rounds, the bytes each GPU sends form a geometric series \(\CollectivePayloadSize/2 + \CollectivePayloadSize/4 + \dots + \CollectivePayloadSize/\DeviceCount = \frac{\DeviceCount-1}{\DeviceCount}\CollectivePayloadSize\), leaving each GPU with a distinct \(\CollectivePayloadSize/\DeviceCount\)-byte slice of the fully reduced result. AllGather traverses the partner bits in reverse, exchanging completed regions of doubling size \(\CollectivePayloadSize/\DeviceCount, 2\CollectivePayloadSize/\DeviceCount, \dots, \CollectivePayloadSize/2\) until every rank holds the full result.

Summing the \(2\log_2 \DeviceCount\) startups and the two symmetric geometric byte series gives the recursive halving-doubling cost: \[ \ButterflyAllReduceTime= 2\log_2 \DeviceCount\cdot \MessageStartupLatency+ 2\frac{\DeviceCount-1}{\DeviceCount} \cdot \frac{\CollectivePayloadSize}{\LinkBandwidth} \]

The schedule combines logarithmic latency (\(\mathcal{O}(\log \DeviceCount)\), like Tree) with bandwidth-optimal data movement (\(2(\DeviceCount-1)/\DeviceCount\cdot \CollectivePayloadSize/\LinkBandwidth\), like Ring). The catch is that it requires nonneighbor communication (GPU \(\RankIndex\) must communicate with GPU \(\RankIndex\oplus 2^\PartnerBitIndex\), which may be physically distant), and it requires \(\DeviceCount\) to be a power of two. For clusters where \(\DeviceCount\) is not a power of two, additional complexity is needed to handle the irregular cases.

Recursive halving-doubling is used in MPI-style collective implementations and is useful as a theoretical point in the latency-bandwidth trade-off (Thakur et al. 2005). NVIDIA’s GPU collective backend, the NVIDIA Collective Communications Library (NCCL), offers implementation families that include ring, tree-derived, hierarchical, NVLink/NVSwitch-aware, and pattern-aware choices, with exact names and availability changing by version and topology (Jeaugey 2017; NVIDIA 2026). Whatever a particular release calls them, practical communication libraries select algorithms through a topology-aware cost model because nonlocal communication patterns can create contention on shared network links that erodes theoretical advantages.

Thakur, Rajeev, Rolf Rabenseifner, and William Gropp. 2005. “Optimization of Collective Communication Operations in MPICH.” The International Journal of High Performance Computing Applications 19 (1): 49–66. https://doi.org/10.1177/1094342005051521.

The partner distance is where topology enters. Consider the ReduceScatter phase for 8 GPUs placed four per node on two nodes, GPUs 0–3 on the first and GPUs 4–7 on the second, which proceeds in \(\log_2(8) = 3\) rounds. In round \(\PartnerBitIndex=0\), each GPU \(\RankIndex\) partners with GPU \(\RankIndex\oplus 1\), pairing neighbors: (0,1), (2,3), (4,5), (6,7). They exchange half their data and reduce. In round \(\PartnerBitIndex=1\), the distance doubles: GPU \(\RankIndex\) partners with GPU \(\RankIndex\oplus 2\), creating pairs (0,2), (1,3), (4,6), (5,7). In round \(\PartnerBitIndex=2\), the distance doubles again: GPU \(\RankIndex\) partners with GPU \(\RankIndex\oplus 4\), creating pairs (0,4), (1,5), (2,6), (3,7).

Physical placement determines the cost of these logical pairings (figure 4). Rounds \(\PartnerBitIndex=0\) and \(\PartnerBitIndex=1\) pair GPUs within a node, but in round \(\PartnerBitIndex=2\) every GPU on the first node exchanges with one on the second, so that round crosses the node boundary. On eight-GPU nodes the same crossing would appear one round later, at \(\PartnerBitIndex=3\) in a 16-rank group. In a separate 1,024-rank example, the final ReduceScatter round pairs GPU \(\RankIndex\) with GPU \(\RankIndex\oplus 512\). If the two rank halves occupy different racks, those exchanges share the inter-rack fabric. The algorithm’s logarithmic rounds and optimal byte volume therefore do not establish its runtime advantage; placement, shared paths, and the changing chunk size in each round must enter the comparison.

5.6.6 Double binary tree

Where recursive halving-doubling requires power-of-two groups and long-distance XOR pairings across fabric cuts, a tree schedule can follow physical topology more flexibly, provided it overcomes the single-tree bandwidth bottleneck. In a single binary tree, half the ranks sit at the leaves and only send (during reduction) or only receive (during broadcast), leaving half of each leaf’s bidirectional link capacity idle while interior nodes do double duty. NCCL resolves this imbalance with a double binary tree (Jeaugey 2017; NVIDIA 2026), constructing two independent binary trees with complementary interior-node roles and running both simultaneously so each carries half the payload.

The complementary-tree construction balances endpoint work by making ranks that act as interior nodes in one tree act as leaves in the other. With eight ranks, build the first tree by labeling the nodes of a binary tree in the order of an in-order traversal (left subtree, then node, then right subtree). In a balanced seven-node tree labeled from 0, in-order traversal puts the leaves at the even ranks 0, 2, 4, and 6 and the interior nodes at 1, 3, and 5. In one simple construction, the eighth rank, 7, joins as one more interior node above that tree. The odd ranks 1, 3, 5, and 7 thus form the first tree’s interior and the even ranks are its leaves. The second tree shifts every rank by one position, so the even ranks become its interior and the odd ranks its leaves (figure 5). Each tree carries half the tensor, \(\CollectivePayloadSize/2\), and the two halves never meet.

Two further steps bring the byte term down from the simple tree’s \(2\log_2 \DeviceCount\cdot \CollectivePayloadSize/\LinkBandwidth\) to about \(2\CollectivePayloadSize/\LinkBandwidth\). First, each tree pipelines its half in small chunks, so while one chunk moves up from the leaves, the next is already leaving them and every level works at once. Tree depth then adds startup rounds and a short pipeline fill, not repeated full-message transfers. Second, count what one rank sends during the reduce phase. As an interior node of one tree it combines the inputs of its (at most two) children and forwards one \(\CollectivePayloadSize/2\) stream to its parent. As a leaf of the other tree it sends its own \(\CollectivePayloadSize/2\) contribution. It therefore sends about \(\CollectivePayloadSize\) in total. The broadcast phase mirrors that traffic, for about \(2\CollectivePayloadSize\) per rank across both phases, the same per-rank volume as the ring. This logical balance does not guarantee that every physical link stays busy or that both trees avoid shared fabric bottlenecks; rank placement and routing still matter.

The combined performance is approximately: \[ \DoubleTreeAllReduceTime\approx 2\log_2 \DeviceCount\cdot \MessageStartupLatency+ \frac{2\CollectivePayloadSize}{\LinkBandwidth} \]

The ideal bandwidth term approaches \(2\CollectivePayloadSize/\LinkBandwidth\) while the startup-round count remains logarithmic. This combination makes the double binary tree a useful candidate across message sizes that penalize either the ring’s many rounds or the simple tree’s repeated full-message transfers. Complementary endpoint roles do not ensure disjoint physical paths, so topology-aware tree construction seeks a good placement, and measurements must establish the achieved rate under contention.

5.6.7 The algorithm crossover point

Plotting the three cost models for a 256-GPU group on a 200 Gbps fabric, over message sizes from 1 KB to 10 GB, shows the simple Tree and Ring curves intersecting near 1 MB (figure 6). At small sizes, fewer startup rounds favor the tree schedules; at large sizes, the simple full-message Tree pays more byte-transfer time. The Double Binary Tree curve includes illustrative overhead factors, so it approaches a comparable bandwidth-dominated regime without representing a measured library result. The candidate schedules differ in their latency and bandwidth terms, in whether they reach the bandwidth lower bound, and in the constraint each one places on the group (table 6).

The intersection near 1 MB in figure 6 marks where the modeled Ring and simple Tree have equal total cost. It is an algorithm crossover, distinct from the startup-versus-transfer crossover for one message. Setting \(\RingAllReduceCompletionTime = \TreeAllReduceCompletionTime\) and solving for payload \(\CollectivePayloadSize\) identifies the point where Ring’s lower byte cost compensates for its additional startup rounds:

\[ \RingAllReduceCompletionTime = 2(\DeviceCount-1)\MessageStartupLatency+ 2\frac{\DeviceCount-1}{\DeviceCount}\frac{\CollectivePayloadSize}{\LinkBandwidth} \] \[ \TreeAllReduceCompletionTime = 2\log_2 \DeviceCount\cdot \MessageStartupLatency+ 2\log_2 \DeviceCount\cdot \frac{\CollectivePayloadSize}{\LinkBandwidth} \]

For large \(\DeviceCount\), \((\DeviceCount-1) \approx \DeviceCount\) and \(\frac{\DeviceCount-1}{\DeviceCount} \approx 1\), so the ring pays a latency term proportional to \(\DeviceCount\) while moving each byte close to the bandwidth limit:

\[ \RingAllReduceCompletionTime \approx 2\DeviceCount\MessageStartupLatency+ \frac{2\CollectivePayloadSize}{\LinkBandwidth}, \quad \TreeAllReduceCompletionTime \approx 2\log_2 \DeviceCount\cdot \MessageStartupLatency+ \frac{2\log_2 \DeviceCount\cdot \CollectivePayloadSize}{\LinkBandwidth} \]

The tree has the opposite shape: logarithmic startup cost, but each message byte moves through more stages. Setting the estimates equal isolates the message size where the cheaper latency path stops compensating for the extra bandwidth work:

\[ 2\DeviceCount\MessageStartupLatency+ \frac{2\CollectivePayloadSize}{\LinkBandwidth} = 2\log_2 \DeviceCount\cdot \MessageStartupLatency+ \frac{2\log_2 \DeviceCount\cdot \CollectivePayloadSize}{\LinkBandwidth} \] \[ \frac{2\CollectivePayloadSize}{\LinkBandwidth} - \frac{2\log_2 \DeviceCount\cdot \CollectivePayloadSize}{\LinkBandwidth} = 2\log_2 \DeviceCount\cdot \MessageStartupLatency- 2\DeviceCount\MessageStartupLatency\] \[ \frac{2\CollectivePayloadSize}{\LinkBandwidth}(1 - \log_2 \DeviceCount) = 2\MessageStartupLatency(\log_2 \DeviceCount- \DeviceCount) \]

Since \(\DeviceCount\gg \log_2 \DeviceCount\) for large clusters, \((\log_2 \DeviceCount- \DeviceCount) \approx -\DeviceCount\) and \((1 - \log_2 \DeviceCount) \approx -\log_2 \DeviceCount\), yielding:

\[ \frac{2\CollectivePayloadSize}{\LinkBandwidth}(-\log_2 \DeviceCount) \approx 2\MessageStartupLatency(-\DeviceCount) \] \[ \boxed{\RingTreeCrossoverPayload \approx \frac{\DeviceCount\cdot \MessageStartupLatency\cdot \LinkBandwidth}{\log_2 \DeviceCount}} \]

The log-aware crossover formula is the large-\(\DeviceCount\) approximation to the exact crossover \(\RingTreeCrossoverPayload(\DeviceCount)\) derived in Ring vs. tree vs. recursive halving-doubling. Within this two-algorithm model, Ring wins above the crossover because its byte savings exceed its extra startup cost; the simple Tree wins below it because the startup saving is larger. Optimized trees and topology-dependent rates require separate comparisons.

On the reference cluster, with the chapter’s effective NDR \(\MessageStartupLatency=\) 1.5 μs and \(\LinkBandwidth=\) 50 GB/s across all 1,024 GPUs, the estimate gives \(\RingTreeCrossoverPayload \approx\) \(\frac{1{,}024 \times 1.5\ \MicroUnitPrefix\text{s} \times 50\ \text{GB/s}}{\log_2 1{,}024} = \frac{1{,}024 \times 1.5\ \MicroUnitPrefix\text{s} \times 50\ \text{GB/s}}{10}\) \(\approx\) 7.7 MB. A gradient bucket of tens of megabytes or the full model gradient therefore sits on the ring side of the crossover, while a reduction of a few hundred kilobytes sits on the tree side. The two other configurations in this section depart from that anchor in stated ways. A smaller 256-GPU group on a slower 200 Gbps fabric with \(\MessageStartupLatency= 1\ \MicroUnitPrefix\text{s}\) moves the curves’ intersection down to about 1 MB (figure 6). A 64-GPU group on a still slower, higher-latency fabric places a 1 MB buffer almost exactly on the crossover (notebook 5.3).

NCCL’s algorithm selection is more nuanced than a two-way split. The double binary tree offers logarithmic latency with near-optimal bandwidth, so it competes with both Ring and Tree across a broad range of message sizes. The library also weighs the number of channels (parallel communication streams), the network topology, and whether a hop is inter-node or intra-node. The effective selection logic resembles a multi-way decision indexed by message size, GPU count, and topology type rather than a single crossover point. The crossover formula nevertheless explains the shape of those choices, because every candidate trades startup rounds against bytes moved. The model therefore names a candidate worth benchmarking rather than proving the library wrong, and an override is retained only after measured collective duration and exposed training-step time confirm it.

Training frameworks decide which payloads reach that analysis. PyTorch DistributedDataParallel (DDP) groups gradient tensors into buckets (section 5.10.3), while FSDP groups parameter gathers and gradient reductions according to its sharding units. A bucket size alone does not locate a crossover, because rank count, effective startup cost, and available bandwidth also enter the comparison. NCCL’s algorithm selection must therefore be evaluated for the actual invocation and topology rather than inferred from a nominal bucket-size range. A single 1 MB buffer on a slower fabric shows how close to the crossover a real bucket can sit.

Napkin Math 5.3: The ring vs. tree crossover
Problem: A cluster synchronizes a 1 MB buffer across 64 GPUs. The network has latency \(\MessageStartupLatency=\) 10 μs and bandwidth \(\LinkBandwidth=\) 10 GB/s. Which algorithm performs better: Ring or Tree?

Math:

Latency:

  1. Ring Latency: \(2(\DeviceCount-1)\MessageStartupLatency=\) \(2 \times 63 \times 10\ \MicroUnitPrefix\text{s}\) = 1260 μs.
  2. Tree Latency: \(2(\log_2 \DeviceCount)\MessageStartupLatency=\) \(2 \times 6 \times 10\ \MicroUnitPrefix\text{s}\) = 120 μs.

Bandwidth (note the difference):

  1. Ring Bandwidth: \(2\frac{\DeviceCount-1}{\DeviceCount}\frac{\CollectivePayloadSize}{\LinkBandwidth} \approx\) \(2 \times \frac{1\text{ MB}}{10\text{ GB/s}}\) = 196.9 μs (optimal: each rank sends about \(2\CollectivePayloadSize\) in total, the lower bound).
  2. Tree Bandwidth: \(2\log_2 \DeviceCount\cdot \frac{\CollectivePayloadSize}{\LinkBandwidth} =\) \(12 \times \frac{1\text{ MB}}{10\text{ GB/s}}\) = 1200 μs (each level sends full message).

Summing the latency and bandwidth terms gives each algorithm’s total time (table 7).

Tree wins, but only by 9 percent. For this 1 MB message, the payload is near the crossover point.

Systems insight: Ring’s latency term (10.5× Tree’s) nearly balances Tree’s bandwidth term (6.1× Ring’s). The log-aware crossover estimate predicts \(\RingTreeCrossoverPayload \approx \DeviceCount\MessageStartupLatency\LinkBandwidth/ \log_2 \DeviceCount=\) \(64 \times 10\ \MicroUnitPrefix\text{s} \times 10\ \text{GB/s} / 6\) \(\approx\) 1.1 MB. At 1 MB, the payload is just below crossover, so Tree wins. At 10 MB, Ring would dominate.

The crossover point sits inside the range that gradient reductions can traverse as bucket sizes change. In this example, the 1 MB AllReduce lies near crossover, whereas a 10 MB DDP bucket is already above it. A fused bucket of 50–300 MB or the full 140 GB BF16 gradient of a 70B parameters model favors Ring over the simple Tree model even more strongly. These conclusions apply to the stated AllReduce configuration. An MoE token-routing payload of the same size still requires an AllToAll schedule, and optimized tree or hierarchical implementations require their own cost comparison.

Checkpoint 5.3: AllReduce algorithm selection

Verify your understanding of Ring vs. Tree AllReduce trade-offs:

Self-Check: Question

  1. Why does Ring AllReduce achieve the information-theoretic lower bound for bandwidth (\(2 \frac{N-1}{N} \frac{M}{\beta}\)) while a simple unsegmented binary Tree AllReduce incurs a bandwidth penalty of \(\mathcal{O}(\log N \cdot \frac{M}{\beta})\)?

    1. Ring AllReduce distributes uniform chunks (\(M/N\)) across all logical ring edges in each round, whereas the simple unsegmented tree sends full messages through successive levels of a reduction and broadcast.
    2. Ring AllReduce compresses floating-point gradients to 1-bit representations during ring circulation, whereas Tree AllReduce always uses uncompressed FP64 precision.
    3. Tree AllReduce requires an external parameter server node to perform floating-point additions, whereas Ring AllReduce computes sums purely in network switch ASICs.
    4. Ring AllReduce eliminates the latency term \(\alpha\) entirely by using asynchronous token rings, whereas Tree AllReduce scales latency quadratically with cluster size.

  2. An infrastructure engineer evaluates AllReduce algorithm selection for synchronizing gradients across a 256-GPU cluster with per-node launch latency \(\alpha = 5\,\mu\text{s}\) and link bandwidth \(\beta = 50\text{ GB/s}\). Using the log-aware crossover model \(M_{\text{crossover}} \approx \frac{N \alpha \beta}{\log_2 N}\), within the simple full-message Tree versus Ring comparison, what is the crossover message size, and which modeled candidate has lower cost for a 500 KB activation tensor versus a 100 MB gradient bucket?

    1. The crossover is \(M_{\text{crossover}} \approx 1.25\text{ GB}\); Tree AllReduce should be selected for both tensors because \(\log_2(256) = 8\) is always smaller than 256.
    2. The crossover is \(M_{\text{crossover}} \approx 800\text{ KB}\); Ring AllReduce should be selected for 500 KB, and Tree AllReduce for 100 MB.
    3. The crossover is \(M_{\text{crossover}} \approx 16\text{ GB}\); Butterfly AllReduce should be selected for both tensors regardless of network topology.
    4. The crossover is \(M_{\text{crossover}} \approx \frac{256 \times (5 \times 10^{-6}\text{ s}) \times (50 \times 10^9\text{ B/s})}{8} = 8\text{ MB}\); Tree AllReduce wins for the 500 KB tensor (latency-dominated), while Ring AllReduce wins for the 100 MB bucket (bandwidth-dominated).

  3. Recursive halving-doubling (Butterfly AllReduce) combines logarithmic startup rounds with bandwidth-optimal byte volume in a flat-network model. What physical placement condition can defeat that modeled advantage, and why do double-tree or hierarchical alternatives still require measurement?

  4. Explain the structural mechanism of the Double Binary Tree AllReduce algorithm that allows it to achieve near-optimal bandwidth (\(\approx 2M/\beta\)) while preserving logarithmic latency (\(\mathcal{O}(\log N)\alpha\)).

  5. Sequence the execution steps of a 4-GPU Ring AllReduce (\(0 \to 1 \to 2 \to 3 \to 0\)) reducing a 4-chunk vector \([A, B, C, D]\): (1) each GPU forwards its fully reduced chunk around the ring for \(N-1\) steps until all ranks possess the complete array, (2) rank \(i\) owns a single fully aggregated chunk of the global sum, (3) each GPU partitions its local vector into \(N=4\) equal chunks, (4) each GPU simultaneously sends chunk \(k\) to its right neighbor and receives from its left neighbor, accumulating partial sums over \(N-1\) steps.

  6. Under the standard balanced point-to-point model, an AllReduce with \(M\) bytes per rank has a per-rank sent-volume lower bound of \(2(N-1)M/N\) bytes. Meeting this bound is known as ____ optimality, which Ring AllReduce achieves.

5.7 Hierarchical Communication

The AllReduce cost models in section 5.6 assume a flat network with uniform endpoint bandwidth, whereas real clusters expose the tiered bandwidth hierarchy of Bandwidth hierarchy. Because intra-node links are abundant and fast while inter-node links are scarce and expensive, hierarchical communication shrinks payloads locally before they cross the scarce tier (decomposing AllReduce into a local ReduceScatter, a cross-node reduction, and a local AllGather) and aligns traffic with the physical fabric, whether switches aggregate packets in the network, rail-optimized trees separate GPU lanes, or direct-connect pods wire accelerators into multidimensional tori.

5.7.1 Hierarchical AllReduce

On a DGX H100 node, each GPU’s local NVLink capacity is 450 GB/s per direction (the 900 GB/s datasheet figure is the bidirectional total), while its one inter-node port provides 50 GB/s over InfiniBand. These are endpoint capacity budgets, not a promise of uniform achieved rate to every peer. Intra-node connectivity and simultaneous traffic still matter. That hierarchical network motivates a schedule that assigns work to each tier according to its usable capacity.

A flat ring whose ordering repeatedly crosses node boundaries can leave local NVLink capacity unused while loading scarce inter-node links. Hierarchical AllReduce explicitly partitions the global operation into three stages that align communication volume with the fabric tiers (figure 7).

With \(\AcceleratorsPerNode\) GPUs in each of \(\ClusterNodeCount\) nodes, the three phases form a single causal pipeline. First, an intra-node ReduceScatter over NVLink performs a local reduction among the \(\AcceleratorsPerNode\) GPUs in each node, leaving each GPU with a \(1/\AcceleratorsPerNode\) shard of partially reduced data while consuming only abundant intra-node bandwidth. Next, each corresponding-rank group applies an inter-node AllReduce over InfiniBand to its \(\CollectivePayloadSize/\AcceleratorsPerNode\)-byte input shard, where the chosen collective algorithm dictates the bytes transmitted across the network fabric. Finally, an intra-node AllGather over NVLink redistributes the globally reduced shards within each node, completing the collective without consuming additional inter-node bandwidth.

Only Phase 2 (Shard AllReduce) loads the slower inter-node InfiniBand links (figure 7). In this middle phase, each corresponding-rank group reduces an \(\CollectivePayloadSize/\AcceleratorsPerNode\)-byte shard across \(\ClusterNodeCount\) nodes. A ring in that group sends \(2(\ClusterNodeCount-1)\CollectivePayloadSize/(\ClusterNodeCount\AcceleratorsPerNode)\) bytes per participating GPU and receives the same amount. Summing across the node’s \(\AcceleratorsPerNode\) GPUs gives \(2(\ClusterNodeCount-1)\CollectivePayloadSize/\ClusterNodeCount\) bytes sent per node. Smaller per-port payloads improve elapsed time when the node has independent ports and sufficient fabric capacity; sharding does not create bandwidth on a shared uplink. An 8-node budget quantifies the bandwidth multiplier this decomposition provides when those ports operate in parallel.

Napkin Math 5.4: The hierarchical bandwidth multiplier
Problem: A cluster has 8 nodes, each with 8 GPUs (64 total). The system must AllReduce the chapter’s 1 GB reference buffer. Assume each GPU has its own full-rate inter-node port, one rail per GPU as on a DGX H100 (rails are the subject of section 5.7.4.1), with no oversubscription. The flat baseline is a single ring over all 64 ranks. How much faster is Hierarchical AllReduce than that flat ring?

Flat ring AllReduce (ignoring hierarchy):

  • Each GPU sends ~2 GB total (the bandwidth-optimal Ring AllReduce formula).
  • Every edge of a ring carries the same traffic in every step, so the whole ring advances at the rate of its slowest edge. Every ordering puts at least one fabric-crossing edge on each node, even the node-contiguous ordering that crosses the fabric only once per node.
  • Effective Bandwidth: Limited by the slowest link = 50 GB/s (InfiniBand).
  • Time: \(\approx\) \(2 \times 1\ \text{GB} / 50\ \text{GB/s}\) = 40 ms (bandwidth term dominates).

Hierarchical AllReduce (3-step decomposition):

  1. Intra-node ReduceScatter: Each GPU sends 875 MB at 450 GB/s per direction → ~1.94 ms

  2. Inter-Node AllReduce: Each GPU AllReduces a \(1\ \text{GB}/8\) = 125 MB shard over InfiniBand; Ring moves roughly \(2(\ClusterNodeCount-1)/\ClusterNodeCount\) times that payload, and after adding the small latency term the phase takes ~4.40 ms at 50 GB/s

    (The eight shard groups run concurrently, each on its own GPU’s port, so the node’s eight ports together carry the full payload in parallel.)

  3. Intra-node AllGather: Each GPU receives 875 MB at 450 GB/s per direction → ~1.94 ms

  4. Total time: \(\approx\) 1.94 ms + 4.40 ms + 1.94 ms = 8.3 ms

Systems insight: Hierarchical AllReduce achieves a 4.8× speedup by reducing the inter-node payload from 1 GB to 125 MB per GPU before the Ring traffic multiplier. The flat ring used one port’s worth of fabric bandwidth because a single ring runs at its slowest edge; the hierarchical schedule uses all eight ports at once. That is also its precondition, since a node whose GPUs shared one uplink would carry the eight shard streams one after another and lose most of the speedup. This is why NVIDIA’s NCCL and similar libraries often select hierarchical algorithms on multi-node clusters when the topology supports them.

This book’s running instance makes the same comparison on the cluster it was defined for. Archetype A’s 175B-parameter model produces a 350 GB BF16 gradient, and the reference cluster holds 1,024 GPUs as 128 nodes of 8. A single flat ring over every GPU would spend about 14.0 s per full-gradient reduction. The hierarchical schedule sends one-eighth of the gradient through each GPU’s port in a shard ring that spans every node and completes in about 3.1 s, a 4.5× improvement. The cross-node phase still accounts for about 1.7 s of that time. Seconds per step remain a large exposed cost even after hierarchy. That is why the production layout for this instance shards the model so that each data-parallel group reduces a fraction of this payload. It is also why the overlap and compression techniques later in the chapter still matter at this scale.

These three phases also generalize to additional fabric tiers. For example, 4 racks of 4 nodes with 8 GPUs per node contain 128 GPUs. A rack-local ReduceScatter over 32 GPUs can leave each GPU with \(1/32\) of the original payload for the cross-rack phase. The total elapsed time still includes both local phases, the cross-rack collective’s traffic factor, startup, and contention. A cross-rack oversubscription ratio alone cannot determine a speedup over a flat ring.

5.7.2 Multidimensional reduction on a torus

Hierarchy fits a fabric whose tiers differ in speed. A torus, the direct-connect topology of Google’s Tensor Processing Unit (TPU) pods, has uniform links instead, and its collectives reduce along one dimension at a time. TPU v4 pods support a 3D torus topology, unlike the 2D torus used by TPU v2 and v3. In the regular 3D torus model, each chip links directly to six neighbors \((\pm \Glyph{X}, \pm \Glyph{Y}, \pm \Glyph{Z})\) with equal per-link bandwidth. For collectives, the torus invites dimension-ordered reduction, which decomposes the AllReduce into sequential Ring AllReduces along axis-aligned torus rings. For a pod with \(\TorusFirstAxisChipCount{\times}\TorusSecondAxisChipCount{\times}\TorusThirdAxisChipCount\) chips, ranks first execute independent X-dimension rings for each fixed \((\Glyph{Y},\Glyph{Z})\) coordinate, producing partial sums along each X line. A second phase runs Y-dimension rings to combine those partials across the \(\Glyph{X} \times \Glyph{Y}\) plane. A third phase executes Z-dimension rings across fixed \((\Glyph{X},\Glyph{Y})\) coordinates to complete the global reduction.

↩︎ The torus topology and its bisection and path-length trade-offs against the fat-tree are established in Dragonfly and torus alternatives.

On a \(4{\times}4\) 2D torus grid of 16 ranks, the dimension-ordered schedule runs in two phases (figure 8). In Phase 1 (orange solid paths), each row executes an independent 4-rank Ring AllReduce across the horizontal X dimension using dashed wrap-around boundary links to close the ring (\(2(\TorusFirstAxisChipCount-1) = 6\) steps). Once row partial sums are established, Phase 2 (slate solid paths) executes orthogonal 4-rank vertical Ring AllReduces down the Y dimension (\(2(\TorusSecondAxisChipCount-1) = 6\) steps). These phases use direct nearest-neighbor links without multi-hop routing within a logical ring edge. They reduce the startup count from \(2(\DeviceCount-1) = 30\) rounds on a flat 16-rank ring to \(2(\TorusFirstAxisChipCount-1) + 2(\TorusSecondAxisChipCount-1) = 12\) rounds, a 2.5× reduction in the modeled startup term. In this simple form each axis still processes the full message, so the round-count saving comes at a byte cost that the partitioned dimension-wise schedule removes.

The byte term does not shrink with the rings. Because every axis processes the full \(\CollectivePayloadSize\)-byte tensor, the bandwidth time is \(2\CollectivePayloadSize[(\TorusFirstAxisChipCount-1)/\TorusFirstAxisChipCount+ (\TorusSecondAxisChipCount-1)/\TorusSecondAxisChipCount+ (\TorusThirdAxisChipCount-1)/\TorusThirdAxisChipCount]/\LinkBandwidth\) for equal per-axis bandwidth \(\LinkBandwidth\). The startup term is \(2[(\TorusFirstAxisChipCount-1)+(\TorusSecondAxisChipCount-1)+(\TorusThirdAxisChipCount-1)]\MessageStartupLatency\). Shorter rings reduce startup cost, but these sequential full-message AllReduces do not retain the bandwidth cost of one global ring. A bandwidth-efficient multidimensional implementation instead partitions the work with dimension-wise ReduceScatter followed by reverse-order AllGather. The X-dimension ReduceScatter sends \(\CollectivePayloadSize(\TorusFirstAxisChipCount-1)/\TorusFirstAxisChipCount\) bytes per rank and leaves each rank at a given X coordinate holding the row-reduced sum for the same \(\CollectivePayloadSize/\TorusFirstAxisChipCount\) shard. Because all ranks in a Y-column share that X coordinate, they hold matching shards and can run a Y-dimension ReduceScatter on that \(\CollectivePayloadSize/\TorusFirstAxisChipCount\) slice alone, sending \((\CollectivePayloadSize/\TorusFirstAxisChipCount)(\TorusSecondAxisChipCount-1)/\TorusSecondAxisChipCount\) bytes per rank and subdividing the slice to \(\CollectivePayloadSize/(\TorusFirstAxisChipCount\TorusSecondAxisChipCount)\). The Z-dimension ReduceScatter repeats the subdivision across ranks that share both X and Y coordinates, leaving each of the \(\DeviceCount= \TorusFirstAxisChipCount \TorusSecondAxisChipCount \TorusThirdAxisChipCount\) chips with a distinct \(\CollectivePayloadSize/\DeviceCount\) shard of the global sum after sending \(\CollectivePayloadSize(\DeviceCount-1)/\DeviceCount\) bytes in total. The mirrored AllGather along Z, Y, and X doubles that volume to \(2(\DeviceCount-1)\CollectivePayloadSize/\DeviceCount\), the ring’s optimal byte term, while keeping the short rings’ startup count. The torus thus teaches the same lesson as hierarchy, which is to shrink the payload before each successive stage rather than send the full tensor through every one.

Dimension-ordered reduction follows direct neighbor links and separates axis traffic in time; it does not change the network diameter or eliminate contention with other workloads. The torus topology also provides natural fault tolerance through alternate routing paths. If one link in the X-dimension fails, traffic can detour through the Y or Z dimensions (at the cost of increased latency). The compiler and collective runtime must choose a schedule for the actual topology, message size, and available links.

5.7.3 In-network reduction with SHARP

Hierarchical and dimension-ordered schedules redistribute work across links, but their software reduction stages still require endpoint GPUs to receive and combine contributions. NVIDIA’s Scalable Hierarchical Aggregation and Reduction Protocol (SHARP)6 moves that intermediate aggregation into the network. Instead of routing each partial sum through a GPU for arithmetic, an aggregation-capable InfiniBand switch combines contributions as packets pass through it.

6 SHARP (Scalable Hierarchical Aggregation and Reduction Protocol): In-network computing on Quantum InfiniBand switches that performs reduction operations (such as sum, min, and max) as packets traverse an aggregation tree (Graham et al. 2020). The same work reports substantially higher reduction bandwidth than host-based algorithms and describes switch-resource limits on outstanding aggregation groups and trees, which can create contention when many collectives compete for the same in-network resources.

Graham, R. L., D. Bureddy, P. Lui, H. Rober, G. Bloch, G. Shainer, J. Poole, et al. 2020. “Scalable Hierarchical Aggregation and Reduction Protocol (SHARP) Streaming-Aggregation Hardware Design and Evaluation.” International Conference on High Performance Computing, 41–59. https://doi.org/10.1007/978-3-030-50743-5_3.

A centralized endpoint reduction and in-switch aggregation send different data up the switch tree (figure 9). In the endpoint tree of panel (a), intermediate switches merely forward raw packets, forcing all four full gradient tensors (\(\LocalGradient_0\) through \(\LocalGradient_3\)) across the upper links into a single Reducer GPU that executes every addition. In panel (b), SHARP offloads arithmetic to aggregation-capable switch application-specific integrated circuits (ASICs): each intermediate switch computes partial sums (\(\LocalGradient_0+\LocalGradient_1\) and \(\LocalGradient_2+\LocalGradient_3\)) in-flight, leaving only the combined shards for the root switch to finalize. Moving reduction into the network fabric halves the upper link traffic and relieves accelerator endpoints from coordinating arithmetic steps.

SHARP changes both terms of the cost model. On the startup side, it shortens the dependency chain. In a software-based tree reduction, each level waits for an intermediate GPU to receive, reduce, and forward its inputs. With SHARP, the switch performs that step in the packet path, so no GPU execution stage or extra endpoint round trip sits between levels. On the byte side, each upper-tier link carries one partial sum rather than every contribution beneath it (figure 9). In the idealized aggregation tree, each GPU sends its contribution up once and receives the result once, whereas a ring rank sends nearly \(2\CollectivePayloadSize\). Endpoint memory traffic falls for the same reason. Switch processing and contention for aggregation resources add costs of their own, so moving arithmetic into the network changes the \(\MessageStartupLatency\)-\(\LinkBandwidth\) terms rather than eliminating them.

Which benefit matters follows the regimes of section 5.5. For small and medium messages, where startup rounds dominate, the shorter dependency chain is the main gain, although for very small messages fixed switch-processing overhead can limit it. For large messages the startup saving becomes proportionally small, and what remains is the byte saving at the endpoints and on the upper tier, which shortens the collective only when those links are the binding resource. Graham et al. (2020) report multi-fold reduction-bandwidth improvements and smaller application-level gains for PyTorch workloads when SHARP Streaming-Aggregation is enabled on HDR/Quantum InfiniBand switches.

Strong scaling campaigns (training a fixed model across more accelerators without proportionally growing batch size) expose the importance of this distinction between collective speed and step time. As the per-accelerator batch shrinks, each gradient tensor stays large (proportional to model size) but the backward pass per accelerator shortens, exposing more of the communication budget. The limiting term can still be bandwidth; strong scaling alone does not establish that startup latency dominates. In-switch aggregation helps when its reduction in endpoint work or fabric traffic shortens the exposed critical path.

In-switch reduction also brings hardware and capacity constraints of its own. The switch ASIC must support the exact reduction operator and numerical format the job uses, such as FP16 or BF16 gradients, which vary across switch generations. Its finite internal memory limits how many aggregation trees can remain active at once. A large cluster running many concurrent jobs or process groups can therefore exhaust switch-tree capacity and fall back to host-based reductions. Moreover, the HDR and Quantum SHARP implementations examined here depend on InfiniBand switch support, so their gains do not carry over to Ethernet fabrics unless an equivalent in-network aggregation path is present.

5.7.4 Topology-aware routing

Two runs of the same 64-GPU Ring AllReduce on identical hardware can differ by a multiple in throughput depending on whether consecutive logical ranks sit on the same NVLink domain or across shared inter-node uplinks. Hierarchy and in-network aggregation change where bytes move and where sums are computed, but their benefit still depends on mapping the logical communication pattern onto physical links.

Communication libraries like NCCL perform topology detection at initialization and use graph search to choose paths through the hardware topology available to them (NVIDIA 2026). For each pair of ranks, the library must determine whether the peer sits behind a local NVLink switch or 100 meters away across an InfiniBand fabric, because that mapping decides which physical links each logical hop occupies. Local GPU, NVLink, PCIe, and NIC connectivity informs those choices, but local discovery does not reveal every remote switch, route, or competing flow. Fabric configuration and placement information must complement the library’s view when diagnosing cross-node contention.

The topology detection process begins with hardware enumeration. NCCL queries the PCIe bus to discover GPU placement, NVLink connectivity between GPUs, and NIC-to-PCIe-switch affinity. From this information, it constructs an internal graph where nodes represent GPUs and edges represent physical links with annotated bandwidth and latency. A graph search algorithm then finds communication paths that maximize aggregate bandwidth while minimizing the number of cross-domain hops (transitions between NVLink, PCIe, and InfiniBand domains). This topology-aware path selection is what allows NCCL to approach theoretical peak bandwidth on well-configured systems, while topology-unaware implementations can leave a large fraction of bandwidth unused.

Rail-optimized routing (NVIDIA DGX)

In the eight-GPU DGX layout considered here, each GPU has a dedicated inter-node NIC, and the fabric connects matching NIC positions to rail switches. Rail-optimized routing fits the corresponding-shard phase of hierarchical AllReduce: GPU 0 on Node A exchanges its shard with GPU 0 on Nodes B and C, GPU 1 exchanges with peer GPU 1s, and so on. The eight rails can carry these groups concurrently (table 8), subject to their physical capacity and competing traffic. Other collectives may require cross-rail paths.

↩︎ Rail-optimized cabling, which connects each GPU slot position to its own rail switch, is established in Rail-optimized topology.

If all eight shard streams converge on one remote NIC, that endpoint faces 8\(\times\) the balanced receive demand. In the equal-shard layout, matching each shard group to its intended rail distributes one-eighth of the payload to each NIC. This removes that particular concentration; achieving the predicted rate still requires sufficient switch, uplink, and bisection capacity for concurrent traffic.

NVIDIA designed the DGX node and rail layout together so that Step 2 of hierarchical AllReduce (section 5.7.1) maps each corresponding-rank shard group onto its own rail. NCCL exploits that structure whenever its topology detection finds aligned ranks. When the job scheduler assigns process ranks without topology awareness, however, logical shards and physical rails drift apart. Cross-node traffic is then routed through the wrong NICs or across spine switches, cutting a node’s usable injection bandwidth to as little as one-eighth of the rail-aligned value when all eight shard exchanges converge on one NIC. Large deployments therefore enforce topology-aware rank assignment through device ordering (CUDA_VISIBLE_DEVICES), NIC binding, and scheduler placement policies so that logical rank \(\LocalRankIndex\) on every node stays bound to rail \(\LocalRankIndex\).

↪ How the scheduler places a job’s gangs on the topology hierarchy so that its collectives stay on fast links is developed in Topology-Aware Scheduling.

Hierarchical execution and topology-aware routing connect the cost model to a physical placement. The \(\MessageStartupLatency\)-\(\LinkBandwidth\) terms estimate the service required by each scheduled exchange; hierarchy redistributes work across fabric tiers, and rail alignment can balance corresponding-shard traffic across independent ports. None removes shared-capacity limits. Measured collective duration must confirm that the intended placement shortens the dependency path before the design is accepted.

Self-Check: Question

  1. On an 8-node cluster with 8 GPUs per node, fast local NVLink, and eight independent 50 GB/s network ports per node, how can hierarchical AllReduce improve on a flat ring whose inter-node traffic is serialized through one port?

    1. Hierarchical AllReduce converts the floating-point values to 8-bit integers during the intra-node phase to save inter-node bandwidth.
    2. Flat Ring AllReduce requires every GPU to establish an optical fiber connection directly to all 63 other GPUs in the cluster.
    3. Local ReduceScatter creates one 125 MB shard per GPU from a 1 GB tensor; corresponding-rank AllReduces can distribute these shards across the eight ports, followed by local AllGather.
    4. Hierarchical AllReduce executes without any inter-node network synchronization barriers.

  2. How does dimension-ordered reduction on a 3D torus network (such as Google TPU pods) optimize communication latency and link utilization compared to running a single global logical ring?

    1. It skips communication along the Z-axis by projecting all activations onto a 2D plane.
    2. It executes axis-aligned rings in sequence, reducing startup rounds to \(2[(X-1)+(Y-1)+(Z-1)]\) while trading extra full-message transfers for shorter rings.
    3. It broadcasts full gradient tensors directly to all pod nodes simultaneously over optical circuit switches.
    4. It routes all collective packets through a single central root TPU in the pod to minimize total wire length.

  3. Describe the architectural principle of rail-optimized routing in multi-GPU DGX clusters, and explain the performance pathology that occurs if a job scheduler assigns process ranks without topology awareness.

  4. True or False: On a supported aggregation path, SHARP can move intermediate reduction arithmetic from endpoints into switches, but the resulting latency and training-step benefit still depend on message size, placement, switch resources, and exposed dependencies.

  5. Sequence the three phases of Hierarchical AllReduce executed across a multi-node cluster with \(G\) GPUs per node: (1) intra-node AllGather across local GPUs over NVLink to reconstruct the full globally reduced tensor, (2) inter-node AllReduce across corresponding GPU positions over InfiniBand on the \(M/G\) node shards, (3) intra-node ReduceScatter across local GPUs over NVLink leaving each GPU holding \(1/G\) of the partially reduced tensor.

See Answers →

5.8 AllToAll Exchanges at Scale

Hierarchy, in-network reduction, and rail alignment were all developed for AllReduce, whose uniform payload lets every rank follow the same schedule. In a mixture-of-experts model with 64 experts distributed across 8 GPUs (8 experts per GPU), or in the sharded embedding tables of table 1, a gating network or lookup table assigns each input to a specific destination rank. Tokens assigned to remote GPUs must physically cross the fabric before expert computation can proceed and return afterward, breaking AllReduce’s symmetry so the exchange finishes only when its slowest path completes.

Systems Perspective 5.1: AllToAll vs. AllReduce: Why scale differs
Ring AllReduce can pipeline a uniform payload through neighbor exchanges. AllToAll presents a different scaling problem because every rank has destination-specific traffic, but the logical peer count alone does not determine its cost.

An AllToAll over \(\DeviceCount\) ranks creates \(\DeviceCount(\DeviceCount-1)\) logical source-destination transfers, yet each rank’s aggregate payload can remain fixed under weak scaling. Its lower bound is governed by the largest of four terms: source injection time, destination reception time, traffic crossing the fabric bisection, and per-message startup or scheduling overhead. Oversubscription and routing determine how much physical capacity those flows share. Message granularity determines how often they pay \(\MessageStartupLatency\). Routing skew determines whether one destination or link becomes the critical path. A full-bisection network carrying balanced, coarse-grained traffic can therefore scale well despite the quadratic logical connection count, whereas an oversubscribed fabric or skewed expert assignment can stall at much smaller \(\DeviceCount\).

Expert parallelism and large-scale recommendation systems can reach a communication wall earlier than standard data parallelism when their traffic exhausts injection, reception, or bisection capacity, or when routing and destination skew create hotspots. The primitive determines the traffic matrix; the physical fabric and routing distribution determine the scaling ceiling.

Logical pairs grow quadratically while per-rank payload can stay flat.

Pricing that 64-expert, 8-GPU MoE layer under uniform routing establishes the baseline transfer volume and injection bound before dynamic skew enters.

Napkin Math 5.5: AllToAll for MoE token routing
Problem: An MoE model processes a batch of 4096 tokens across 8 GPUs (512 tokens per GPU). Each token is a 2048-dimensional hidden state in BF16 (4.096 KB per token). The gating network assigns each token to exactly 1 of 64 experts (8 experts per GPU). Assuming uniform routing (each expert receives 64 tokens), how much data does each GPU send and receive?

Math:

Each GPU holds 512 tokens that need to reach 64 different experts across 8 GPUs. With uniform routing, each GPU sends 64 tokens to each of the other 7 GPUs (keeping 64 tokens local).

  • Data per GPU-to-GPU transfer: 64 tokens \(\times\) 4.096 KB/token = 262.144 KB
  • Total data sent per GPU: 7 \(\times\) 262.144 KB = 1.84 MB
  • Total data received per GPU: 1.84 MB (symmetric)

Latency Analysis (InfiniBand NDR, \(\MessageStartupLatency=\) 1.5 μs, \(\LinkBandwidth=\) 50 GB/s):

Each of the 7 transfers is a 262.144 KB message. From the \(\MessageStartupLatency\)-\(\LinkBandwidth\) model: \(\TransferTimeForPayload\) = 1.5 μs + 5 μs = 6.5 μs per transfer.

If serialized: 7 \(\times\) 6.5 μs = 45.5 μs. If all 7 transfers are issued concurrently, they still share the GPU’s 50 GB/s injection port. The aggregate injection lower bound is therefore \(\MessageStartupLatency+ \text{total bytes sent}/\LinkBandwidth=\) 38.2 μs, before contention and additional collective overhead. Full duplex permits simultaneous sending and receiving; it does not provide one full-bandwidth outgoing port per peer.

Systems insight: The injection result is only a lower bound. If the topology’s usable bisection bandwidth is \(\BisectionBandwidth\) and \(\CutCrossingDataVolume\) bytes must cross its minimum cut, the exchange also obeys \(\AllToAllExchangeTime\geq \CutCrossingDataVolume/\BisectionBandwidth\). Oversubscription reduces \(\BisectionBandwidth\), fine-grained peer messages add startup cost, and expert skew raises the maximum receive volume above the uniform estimate. The forward pass runs the AllToAll twice per layer, once for token dispatch and once for result collection; training also communicates gradients during backward.

5.8.1 Dynamic routing and expert imbalance in MoE AllToAll

The worked trace in notebook 5.5 assumes uniform token routing, in which every expert receives an identical share of tokens. In production MoE models, routing decisions are dynamic and data-dependent. The exchange keeps its two-phase shape, but its buffer sizes, its slowest receiver, and its load on the fabric all become functions of the routing distribution. The hierarchy just developed for AllReduce reappears as one of the schedules that can contain them.

Each MoE layer performs two exchanges. In an MoE transformer layer with \(\ExpertCount\) experts distributed across \(\DeviceCount\) ranks (\(\ExpertCount/\DeviceCount\) experts per rank), each rank holds a local batch of \(\LocalTokenCount\) tokens. A gating router computes top-\(\RoutedExpertCount\) routing probabilities over all \(\ExpertCount\) experts for each token, inducing two collective communication phases separated by expert computation. During token dispatch, each rank partitions its \(\LocalTokenCount\) local token activation vectors (\(\LayerInputVector\in \mathbb{R}^\HiddenDimension\), where \(\HiddenDimension\) is the model’s hidden dimension) according to the destination ranks hosting the selected experts. The rank then executes an AllToAll collective (or variable-size AllToAllv) to route tokens across the fabric according to the routing matrix. After each rank computes forward passes through its assigned local expert feed-forward networks (FFNs), the token combine phase executes a second AllToAll collective to route output vectors back to their originating ranks. Each rank then computes the weighted combination of expert outputs: \[\LayerOutputVector_i = \sum_{j \in \text{top-}\RoutedExpertCount} {\RouterGateProbability}_{i,j} \cdot \text{Expert}_j(\LayerInputVector_i)\]

Because routing is data-dependent, so are the required buffer sizes, even when the input tensor shape is fixed.
