One simplified model treats assignments of different tokens independently: the count for expert \(\ExpertIndex\) is then \(\ExpertTokenCount\sim \text{Binomial}(\DeviceCount \LocalTokenCount, \ExpertSelectionProbability)\), where \(\ExpertSelectionProbability\) is the fixed probability that a token selects expert \(\ExpertIndex\). Correlated tokens or changing router probabilities require a different count model. To avoid reallocating buffers for every routing decision, a capacity-limited implementation can reserve space using an expert capacity factor \(\ExpertCapacityFactor\ge 1.0\), a multiplier on each expert’s average share of token assignments (Lepikhin et al. 2021; Fedus et al. 2022): \[\text{Capacity}_\ExpertIndex= \left\lceil \frac{\DeviceCount\times \LocalTokenCount\times \RoutedExpertCount}{\ExpertCount} \times \ExpertCapacityFactor\right\rceil\]

Lepikhin, Dmitry, HyoukJoong Lee, Yuanzhong Xu, Dehao Chen, Orhan Firat, Yanping Huang, Maxim Krikun, Noam Shazeer, and Zhifeng Chen. 2021. “GShard: Scaling Giant Models with Conditional Computation and Automatic Sharding.” International Conference on Learning Representations (ICLR).
Fedus, William, Barret Zoph, and Noam Shazeer. 2022. “Switch Transformers: Scaling to Trillion Parameter Models with Simple and Efficient Sparsity.” Journal of Machine Learning Research 23 (120): 1–39.

The capacity factor exposes a trade-off between reserved space and retained expert assignments. At uniform expected capacity (\(\ExpertCapacityFactor= 1.0\)), some experts can still overflow while others have unused slots. A dropping implementation bypasses excess assignments through the residual connection, changing the computation and potentially harming training quality. Increasing headroom (\(\ExpertCapacityFactor> 1.0\), for example \(1.25\)–\(1.5\)) can reduce overflow. If the implementation transmits every reserved slot, its padded payload grows by approximately a factor of \(\ExpertCapacityFactor\) relative to the uniform capacity-one budget; kernels that skip unused slots need not perform the corresponding dummy computation. A dropless implementation retains every assignment and can use variable-size AllToAllv to avoid sending unused slots. It still needs enough memory for the realized counts and remains exposed to routing skew.

Skew turns the most heavily loaded rank into a straggler. Because token routing is determined by input semantics, some experts inevitably receive disproportionate token volumes. An expert that receives several times the average load raises its host GPU’s receive traffic by the same excess. The host’s total receive volume depends on the combined load of all experts assigned to it, which is the rank-level quantity in the bound that follows.

Routing skew can create stragglers in both communication and expert computation. An inbound reception bottleneck bounds dispatch completion by the most heavily loaded receiver. Other ranks may finish their transfers earlier, but work that needs the delayed expert outputs must wait. The receive budget gives a lower bound before expert computation and the return exchange are counted: \[\TokenDispatchTime\ge \frac{\MaxReceivedTokenCount\times \HiddenDimension\times \ElementStorageSize}{\ReceiveBandwidth}\] where \(\MaxReceivedTokenCount= \max_\RankIndex \sum_{\ExpertIndex\in \text{rank } \RankIndex} \ExpertTokenCount\) counts the largest remotely received token batch at any rank \(\RankIndex\), restricting \(\ExpertTokenCount\) in this sum to assignments arriving from other ranks. Here \(\ElementStorageSize\) is the bytes per activation element, and \(\ReceiveBandwidth\) is that rank’s receive bandwidth. Bisection capacity provides a separate limit. Ring AllReduce exchanges data with logical ring neighbors, whereas AllToAll permits a complete bipartite exchange. Under uniform destination assignment, a fraction \((\DeviceCount-1)/\DeviceCount\approx 1\) of tokens leaves its source rank; the fraction crossing nodes or a particular fabric cut depends on placement. In the simplified symmetric fabric model with oversubscription ratio \(\OversubscriptionRatio:1\), usable bisection bandwidth \(\BisectionBandwidth = (\DeviceCount\cdot \InjectionBandwidth) / (2\OversubscriptionRatio)\) bounds the time needed to carry traffic across that cut: \[\TokenDispatchTime\ge \frac{\CutCrossingDataVolume}{\BisectionBandwidth}\]

Napkin Math 5.6: The cost of a hot expert rank
Problem: Return to the uniform routing of notebook 5.5: 8 GPUs, 64 experts, 4.096 KB per token, and one 50 GB/s port per GPU. Suppose skew sends one rank’s experts 1.5 times the average remote load. This is an assumed realized routing skew; the per-expert capacity factor reserves total-assignment headroom and does not determine remote arrivals. How does the receive bound on dispatch change?

Math:

  1. Uniform receiver: Each GPU receives 448 remote tokens, or 1.84 MB, so \(\TokenDispatchTime\ge\) 1.84 MB / 50 GB/s \(\approx\) 36.7 μs.
  2. Hot receiver: \(\MaxReceivedTokenCount\) rises to 672 remote tokens, or 2.75 MB, so \(\TokenDispatchTime\ge\) 2.75 MB / 50 GB/s \(\approx\) 55.1 μs.

Systems insight: The exchange cannot finish before its hottest receiver, so a 1.5× load on one rank raises the dispatch bound for the whole layer by the same factor. Transmitting unused capacity slots can add padding bytes; the amount depends on the packing layout rather than on this receive-skew factor alone. A variable-size exchange removes unused-slot traffic but not the receive bound. Expert computation processes local as well as remote assignments, so its work grows by the same factor only if the hot rank’s total local-plus-remote assignment count also grows by that factor, with unchanged work per assignment.

Scale compounds both problems. To amortize small-message overhead across thousands of GPUs, hierarchical MoE runtimes decompose the flat AllToAll into a two-level collective (Hwang et al. 2023). Intra-node NVLink first coalesces tokens destined for experts on the same remote server node. Participating nodes then exchange aggregated payloads over InfiniBand or RoCE, and receiving GPUs use NVLink to distribute tokens to the GPUs hosting the targeted experts. At the logical level, this replaces \(\DeviceCount(\DeviceCount-1)\) directed rank pairs with \(\ClusterNodeCount(\ClusterNodeCount-1)\) directed node pairs; actual transport connections depend on the implementation. Coalescing can improve link utilization and reduce startup overhead, but the token bytes still have to cross the required fabric cuts, and skew can still overload a destination.

5.8.2 AllToAll schedule selection

A flat, fixed-size AllToAll schedule that works well for balanced, megabyte-scale peer transfers on eight GPUs can stall across thousands of GPUs when per-peer chunks shrink to a few kilobytes or when expert skew forces every rank to pad its buffers to the hottest receiver’s count. Because AllToAll names only the destination-specific contract, the runtime must match its schedule to the traffic matrix and physical hierarchy rather than treating every exchange as one flat pattern. Three schedules cover the common regimes (table 9). Direct pairwise exchange suits balanced, coarse per-peer chunks. Hierarchical exchange coalesces many small remote chunks before they cross scarce inter-node links. Variable-size exchange avoids padding when token or embedding counts differ by destination. Hierarchical designs such as Tutel’s two-dimensional exchange demonstrate why the best schedule changes with message size, scale, and topology (Hwang et al. 2023).

Hwang, Changho, Wei Cui, Yifan Xiong, Ziyue Yang, Ze Liu, Han Hu, Zilong Wang, et al. 2023. “Tutel: Adaptive Mixture-of-Experts at Scale.” Proceedings of Machine Learning and Systems (MLSys) 5.

The selection test follows the four-term lower bound from perspective 5.1 and section 5.8.1. When per-peer chunks are already coarse and source injection or fabric bisection dominates, coalescing cannot shrink the byte term and only adds local copies. When each remote destination receives a small chunk so that \(\DeviceCount\MessageStartupLatency\) dominates instead, a hierarchical schedule replaces those many inter-node startups with a single aggregated exchange per remote node. Under routing skew, a variable-size interface keeps padding from inflating \(\CutCrossingDataVolume\), though the runtime must still provision memory and time for the hottest receiver’s load. Because these bounds depend on live routing behavior, a runtime must benchmark candidate schedules under the observed token-count distribution, rank placement, and fabric topology rather than relying on a balanced, fixed-size microbenchmark. Even the best schedule, however, must still transmit the full activation or gradient payload unless the system reduces the bytes themselves.

Self-Check: Question

  1. Why can MoE token routing with AllToAll encounter contention and tail-latency limits even when its per-worker payload is smaller than that of a dense AllReduce?

    1. AllToAll transfers more total bytes per GPU than AllReduce, requiring each GPU to send \(2(N-1)M\) bytes instead of \(2 \frac{N-1}{N} M\) bytes.
    2. AllToAll exchanges distinct payloads among many peers; routing skew and concurrent cross-fabric transfers can concentrate traffic on shared links and destinations.
    3. AllToAll cannot execute over InfiniBand fabrics and must fall back to host CPU TCP/IP socket emulation.
    4. AllToAll mandates single-precision FP32 representation, preventing BF16 or FP16 tensor transfers across remote experts.

  2. True or False: In a mixture-of-experts (MoE) model where tokens are routed across 8 GPUs using full-duplex InfiniBand NDR (50 GB/s per port), issuing 7 concurrent peer transfers allows the GPU to achieve an aggregate outgoing transmission rate of \(7 \times 50\text{ GB/s} = 350\text{ GB/s}\).

See Answers →

5.9 Gradient Compression Under Bandwidth Scarcity

Training across data centers over wide-area links or on cloud instances with commodity Ethernet can leave each worker with 10–100\(\times\) less network bandwidth than the reference InfiniBand fabric. Federated training on phone-class devices, the Archetype C (Federated MobileNet) regime, faces cellular or Wi-Fi uplinks that are both slower and intermittently available. Even after hierarchical routing and schedule tuning extract all usable capacity from such links, moving a full-precision gradient or a multi-step client update can still dominate step time. Gradient compression attacks the payload size directly by trading numerical representation fidelity for fewer transmitted bits, offering nominal value-payload reductions of 4–1000\(\times\) before metadata and aggregation costs reduce the realized savings. Because noisy or biased updates can slow or stall optimization, the central engineering question is how much communication volume a compressor can remove while preserving progress toward the training quality target.

↪ The per-round uplink budget of federated training, and the update compression that fits it to cellular and Wi-Fi links, are developed in Bandwidth-aware update compression.

5.9.1 Precision reduction via quantization

Quantization is the least disruptive compression lever when bandwidth is binding because it keeps every gradient element but reduces the bits used to represent it. Most gradients are computed in FP32 (32-bit floating point) or BF16 (16-bit brain float), so reducing bit-width directly reduces communication volume. The useful question is how much gradient fidelity the optimizer can absorb across the progression from mild to aggressive quantization. The compression ratios discussed in this section follow the compression literature and are quoted against FP32 values. The chapter’s 70B payload is already BF16, so against it every ratio halves; INT8 saves 2\(\times\) rather than 4\(\times\), and 1-bit encoding 16\(\times\) rather than 32\(\times\).

FP16 halves FP32’s value bytes when its range supports the workload, and INT8 stores each quantized value in one byte, a \(4\times\) reduction relative to FP32 before scale metadata. A symmetric INT8 scheme uses 255 levels from negative 127 through positive 127. For a nonzero gradient vector, it computes a tensor-wide scaling factor \(\QuantizationScale= \max(\lvert \LocalGradient\rvert) / 127\), quantizes via \(\QuantizedGradient= \text{round}(\LocalGradient/\QuantizationScale)\), and has the receiver reconstruct \(\ReconstructedGradient= \QuantizedGradient\times \QuantizationScale\). An all-zero vector requires a separate zero case. Gradients differ from other quantized tensors in the reduction. Each rank computes its scale from its own gradient, so independently scaled integer vectors cannot be added directly to recover the sum of their reconstructed gradients. The collective must carry every rank’s scale through the reduction and use sufficient accumulator precision, or reconstruct values before reducing them. At the extreme, 1-bit SGD transmits only the sign of each gradient element (\(+1\) or \(-1\)), yielding a \(32\times\) reduction in value bits over FP32. The receiver reconstructs values using a learned or adaptive scaling factor, though the substantial noise introduced by 1-bit quantization can severely degrade convergence without error-feedback mechanisms (Seide et al. 2014; Karimireddy et al. 2019).

↪ Byte widths for every format named here are collected for reference in Numeric Precision and Training Memory.

Block quantization for gradient communication

Using a single scaling factor for an entire tensor makes every element’s quantization resolution depend on the largest magnitude. When magnitudes vary substantially across the tensor, small gradients may round to zero even in regions whose values could be represented accurately with a smaller scale. Block quantization addresses this by dividing the gradient tensor into blocks of \(\QuantizationBlockSize\) elements (for example, \(\QuantizationBlockSize= 64\) or \(128\)) and computing an independent scaling factor per block. Each block’s scaling factor adapts to its local range, improving resolution where that range is smaller at the cost of transmitting \(\GradientElementCount/\QuantizationBlockSize\) additional scaling factors for evenly divided blocks.

For a gradient vector of dimension \(\GradientElementCount\) quantized to INT8 with block size \(\QuantizationBlockSize\), the message size is \(\GradientElementCount\times 1\ \text{byte}\) for the quantized values plus \((\GradientElementCount/\QuantizationBlockSize) \times 4\ \text{bytes}\) for the FP32 scaling factors, or \(\GradientElementCount+ 4\GradientElementCount/\QuantizationBlockSize\) bytes in total. The effective compression is therefore \(4\GradientElementCount/ (\GradientElementCount+ 4\GradientElementCount/\QuantizationBlockSize) = 4\QuantizationBlockSize/ (\QuantizationBlockSize+ 4)\), which for \(\QuantizationBlockSize= 128\) reaches 3.88\(\times\), close to the theoretical 4\(\times\). For rounding to the nearest level without clipping, the absolute error bound changes from \(\QuantizationScale\cdot 0.5\), where \(\QuantizationScale\) is the global scaling factor, to \(\BlockQuantizationScale\cdot 0.5\), where \(\BlockQuantizationScale\) is the block-local scaling factor. Blocks with smaller ranges obtain tighter bounds; the improvement depends on how magnitudes are distributed among blocks.

Interfaces that fuse quantization and reduction must preserve the meaning of each block’s scale throughout aggregation. They must also control the resulting numerical error. Deterministic rounding generally introduces bias, whereas suitable randomized quantizers preserve the input in expectation while adding variance. Error feedback addresses accumulated compression error, but the need for it depends on the compressor and optimizer rather than on bit-width alone. Quantization should therefore move only as far down the precision ladder as the convergence budget permits.

5.9.2 Gradient sparsification methods

Even 1-bit quantization must send at least one bit per coordinate, capping its value-payload reduction at \(16\times\) relative to BF16 (\(32\times\) relative to FP32) before scale metadata. Pushing compression to \(100\times\) or beyond requires an orthogonal lever, sparsification, which keeps full or half precision for selected coordinates and transmits only a small subset of gradient elements while setting the rest to zero.

Top-k sparsification

Top-k compression selects that subset by magnitude: for a gradient vector \(\LocalGradient\in \mathbb{R}^{\GradientElementCount}\), it transmits only the \(\RetainedGradientElementCount\) elements with the largest absolute magnitude, setting the rest to zero (Aji and Heafield 2017; Lin et al. 2018):

\[\text{TopK}(\LocalGradient) = \LocalGradient\odot \TopMagnitudeIndicator\]

where \(|\LocalGradient|_{(\RetainedGradientElementCount)}\) is the \(\RetainedGradientElementCount\)-th largest element by magnitude. The threshold expression retains all ties; an implementation targeting an exact count must break ties at the boundary. With \(\RetainedGradientElementCount= 0.001 \times \GradientElementCount\) (keeping only 0.1 percent of elements), the retained values occupy 1000\(\times\) less space before index overhead.

The compression headline must be discounted by the cost of encoding the sparse representation. Transmitting a sparse gradient requires sending both the nonzero values and their indices. For a gradient vector of dimension \(\GradientElementCount\) with \(\RetainedGradientElementCount\) nonzero elements, the encoded message contains \(\RetainedGradientElementCount\) values (each in FP32 or FP16) plus \(\RetainedGradientElementCount\) indices (typically INT32). The total message size is \(\RetainedGradientElementCount\times (4 + 4) = 8 \RetainedGradientElementCount\) bytes for FP32 values with INT32 indices. The effective compression ratio is therefore \(4\GradientElementCount/ (8 \RetainedGradientElementCount) = \GradientElementCount/ (2 \RetainedGradientElementCount)\).

For \(\RetainedGradientElementCount= 0.001 \GradientElementCount\), the encoded message yields a compression ratio of 500\(\times\), not the 1000\(\times\) suggested by the kept-element fraction alone, because the index overhead is significant. Using a 16-bit unsigned index representation (for \(\GradientElementCount< 65536\)) or run-length encoding for suitable sparsity patterns can improve the effective ratio. Aggregation imposes a separate cost. Workers may retain different coordinates, so summing their packed value arrays would combine unrelated parameters. A sparse collective must match indices and sum contributions to each coordinate; the union of selected coordinates can be much larger than any one worker’s selection. Local compression ratios therefore do not directly specify collective traffic or runtime.

Top-k is not the only sparsification rule. Random-k sparsification selects \(\RandomRetainedElementCount\) elements uniformly at random and scales them by \(\GradientElementCount/\RandomRetainedElementCount\) to maintain an unbiased gradient estimate. Random-k has the advantage of being an unbiased compressor even without error feedback, because \(\mathbb{E}[\text{RandomK}(\LocalGradient)] = \LocalGradient\). Its random selection can discard large components, and rescaling the retained values introduces variance. Top-k retains the largest components but introduces bias. Their convergence behavior therefore depends on the optimizer, residual correction, and gradient distribution; unbiasedness alone does not determine which reaches a quality target sooner.

The convergence problem

Naively discarding small gradients with Top-k creates a systematic bias. If a parameter’s gradient remains below the selection threshold, even a consistent contribution of 0.01 per step is repeatedly omitted. Over thousands of steps, those omissions can materially change the optimization trajectory. The problem is persistent exclusion, which can occur even when each discarded component looks negligible in isolation.

The bias of uncorrected Top-k means that the usual argument for SGD with an unbiased gradient estimate no longer applies directly. Biased compression can cause convergence failure even when the uncompressed method converges. Deterministic quantization can also repeatedly omit small contributions; randomized quantization and rescaled Random-k instead require accounting for their added variance. Error feedback addresses accumulated compression error for quantized gradients (Seide et al. 2014) and for sparsified gradients (Stich et al. 2018; Karimireddy et al. 2019).

5.9.3 Error feedback and residual accumulation

Error feedback prevents that 0.01 sub-threshold component from being lost forever. Rather than discarding unselected or rounded-away gradient coordinates, each worker retains a local residual accumulator \(\ErrorFeedbackResidual_t\). At step \(\OptimizerStepIndex\), the system adds \(\ErrorFeedbackResidual_t\) to the new gradient \(\LocalGradient_t\), compresses the corrected vector, transmits \(\CompressedGradientUpdate_t\), and stores the untransmitted difference as \(\ErrorFeedbackResidual_{t+1}\), allowing persistent small contributions to accumulate until they cross the transmission threshold.

Error feedback carries the residual into the next compressed step.

Definition 5.3: Error feedback mechanism

Error feedback is a distributed-training technique for gradient compression that maintains a per-worker residual accumulator \(\ErrorFeedbackResidual_t\), re-injecting the compression error back into the next gradient update so that information omitted from the transmitted update remains in the residual. A finite run can end with an untransmitted residual.

  1. Significance: Top-k sparsification at 1 percent keeps only the largest 1 percent of gradient values by magnitude, reducing each worker’s retained value payload from 140 GB to 1.4 GB for a full 70B gradient, a 100\(\times\) reduction before sparse-index and aggregation overhead. Without error feedback, the discarded 99 percent of gradient values create a biased update that can harm convergence. With error feedback, omitted information remains in the residual and can influence later selections, supporting the convergence guarantees analyzed for memory/error-feedback variants under their assumptions (Stich et al. 2018; Karimireddy et al. 2019).
  2. Distinction: Unlike plain lossy compression, which permanently discards sub-threshold gradient components, error feedback delays their transmission. A component too small to send in one step stays in the residual and can influence later transmitted updates as it accumulates; a finite run may still end with an untransmitted residual.
  3. Common pitfall: A frequent misconception is that error feedback is automatic whenever a compressor is used. It is not: the training system must explicitly preserve and reinject the residual. Without that residual path, Top-k and 1-bit quantization introduce systematic bias that can accumulate across steps (Karimireddy et al. 2019).

Error feedback preserves cumulative gradient information over time. Consider what happens over \(\TrainingStepCount\) steps:

\[\sum_{t=1}^{\TrainingStepCount} \CompressedGradientUpdate_t = \sum_{t=1}^{\TrainingStepCount} \left[(\LocalGradient_t + \ErrorFeedbackResidual_t) - \ErrorFeedbackResidual_{t+1}\right] = \sum_{t=1}^{\TrainingStepCount} \LocalGradient_t + \ErrorFeedbackResidual_1 - \ErrorFeedbackResidual_{\TrainingStepCount+1}\]

If the error accumulator remains bounded under the chosen compressor and training assumptions, then as \(\TrainingStepCount\to \infty\):

\[\frac{1}{\TrainingStepCount}\sum_{t=1}^{\TrainingStepCount} \CompressedGradientUpdate_t \to \frac{1}{\TrainingStepCount}\sum_{t=1}^{\TrainingStepCount} \LocalGradient_t\]

Under that bounded-residual condition, the difference between the two averages vanishes. At a finite stopping point, untransmitted information remains in the residual. Repeated small gradients can accumulate in \(\ErrorFeedbackResidual_t\) until they exceed the compression threshold, as the worked trace illustrates.

The telescoping property of compression error across time is why error feedback can transform a biased compressor into a convergent training method. Theoretical analyses show convergence guarantees for sparsified SGD with memory and for EF-SGD with broad compression operators under their stated assumptions (Stich et al. 2018; Karimireddy et al. 2019). A rounding compressor with no residual path shows what is lost without that memory (table 10). It transmits each value rounded to the nearest whole unit, so any value below 0.5 is sent as zero.

A residual accumulator \(\ErrorFeedbackResidual_t\) that re-injects the discarded remainder into the next step changes the outcome of the same sequence (table 11). Instead of vanishing at each rounding step, the fractional updates accumulate locally until they cross the transmission threshold.

Example 5.2: Error feedback mechanism
Scenario: A gradient compression pipeline rounds each gradient value to the nearest whole unit before transmission, so values below 0.5 are sent as zero, over 5 training steps of distributed data-parallel training.

Diagnosis: Naive rounding (table 10) discards every value below 0.5, transmitting 0 when the true cumulative gradient is 1.6 (100 percent gradient loss). With error feedback (table 11), the residual grows until it crosses 0.5 and is sent, so the run transmits 2 and ends holding −0.4 in the residual. Transmitted plus residual, 2 + (−0.4), equals the true total of 1.6.

Systems lesson: The trace illustrates how error feedback carries omitted information into later updates. By storing untransmitted residual vectors in local accelerator memory (\(\ErrorFeedbackResidual_{t+1} = \LocalGradient_t + \ErrorFeedbackResidual_t - \CompressedGradientUpdate_t\)), repeated small contributions can accumulate until they cross the transmission threshold. The trace demonstrates the residual mechanism; convergence still depends on the compressor and optimizer assumptions.

5.9.4 1-bit Adam compression-aware optimization

Compressing raw gradients works cleanly for stochastic gradient descent, where the parameter update is linear in the gradient. Adaptive optimizers such as Adaptive Moment Estimation (Adam) divide the first moment (momentum) by the square root of a running second-moment (variance) estimate, so compression noise in the raw gradient distorts that nonlinear denominator and can destabilize training. Microsoft’s DeepSpeed team observed that Adam’s second-moment estimate stabilizes early in training even while momentum continues to evolve (Tang et al. 2021). Rather than compressing raw gradients on every step, 1-bit Adam runs standard full-precision Adam during an initial warmup phase until the variance estimate settles. It then freezes that variance tensor as a fixed adaptive preconditioner and switches to a communication-efficient phase. In this phase, workers communicate only the momentum using 1-bit compression (sign plus a scaling factor) while retaining each compression residual locally for the next step.

Because the frozen preconditioner is no longer perturbed by quantization noise and the local residual feeds omitted momentum back into subsequent messages, the method controls accumulated compression error and preserves Adam’s convergence trajectory without requiring each 1-bit message to be an unbiased estimator. Across experiments up to 256 GPUs, Tang et al. (2021) report up to 5\(\times\) end-to-end communication-volume reduction. That is smaller than the raw 32\(\times\) ratio of 1-bit values to FP32 momentum, because the uncompressed warmup and other communication costs are included. The same work reports convergence speed that matches uncompressed Adam, with up to 3.3\(\times\) higher throughput for BERT-Large pretraining and up to 2.9\(\times\) higher throughput for Stanford Question Answering Dataset (SQuAD) fine-tuning. Designing the compressor around the optimizer’s internal state thus succeeds where replacing every raw gradient with its sign would fail, provided the compression overhead remains small compared with the communication time saved.

5.9.5 Bandwidth versus convergence trade-offs in compression

Across quantization, sparsification, error feedback, and 1-bit Adam, greater byte reduction demands stronger residual or preconditioner safeguards to protect convergence. Placing these methods side by side against both FP32 and BF16 baselines (table 12) maps each compression ratio to the convergence control and link regime it requires (Seide et al. 2014; Aji and Heafield 2017; Lin et al. 2018; Stich et al. 2018; Karimireddy et al. 2019; Tang et al. 2021).

Seide, Frank, Hao Fu, Jasha Droppo, Gang Li, and Dong Yu. 2014. “1-Bit Stochastic Gradient Descent and Its Application to Data-Parallel Distributed Training of Speech DNNs.” Interspeech 2014, 1058–62. https://doi.org/10.21437/interspeech.2014-274.
Aji, Alham Fikri, and Kenneth Heafield. 2017. “Sparse Communication for Distributed Gradient Descent.” Proceedings of the 2017 Conference on Empirical Methods in Natural Language Processing, 440–45. https://doi.org/10.18653/v1/d17-1045.
Lin, Yujun, Song Han, Huizi Mao, Yu Wang, and William J Dally. 2018. “Deep Gradient Compression: Reducing the Communication Bandwidth for Distributed Training.” International Conference on Learning Representations (ICLR).
Stich, S. U., J.-B. Cordonnier, and M. Jaggi. 2018. “Sparsified SGD with Memory.” Advances in Neural Information Processing Systems 31.
Karimireddy, Sai Praneeth, Quentin Rebjock, Sebastian U. Stich, and Martin Jaggi. 2019. “Error Feedback Fixes SignSGD and Other Gradient Compression Schemes.” Proceedings of the 36th International Conference on Machine Learning (ICML), Proceedings of machine learning research, vol. 97: 3252–61.
Tang, Hanlin, Shaoduo Gan, Ammar Ahmad Awan, Samyam Rajbhandari, Conglong Li, Xiangru Lian, Ji Liu, Ce Zhang, and Yuxiong He. 2021. “1-Bit Adam: Communication Efficient Large-Scale Training with Adam’s Convergence Speed.” Proceedings of the 38th International Conference on Machine Learning (ICML), Proceedings of machine learning research, vol. 139: 10118–29.

Decision criteria for gradient compression

The decision rule is not the compression ratio alone. Compression is worthwhile only when the wall-clock time it saves across the run exceeds its encoding overhead plus the cost of any extra steps that slower convergence requires, so the comparison must be made in time to the same quality target. A high communication-computation ratio \(\CommunicationComputeRatio= \CommunicationTime(\DeviceCount)/(\IronLawComputeTime/\DeviceCount)\) creates an opportunity when that communication is exposed on the critical path. The ratio compares communication time with ideally partitioned compute time and separates compute-bound from communication-bound clusters (The communication-computation ratio). The compressor and optimizer analysis must establish how bias or variance is controlled, including whether error feedback is required; convergence tests then check that the chosen configuration reaches the target.

The first test is per synchronization. Compression shrinks only the byte term, so it helps a payload above \(\LatencyBandwidthCrossoverSize\), such as a full gradient or a large bucket, and does little for one below it, whose startup term remains (section 5.5). Even above \(\LatencyBandwidthCrossoverSize\), encoding must cost less than the transfer time it removes.

Napkin Math 5.7: The payback of compression
Problem: A training job takes 40 ms to synchronize a gradient buffer. A compression scheme shrinks the payload, but after scale factors, metadata, and protocol overhead its assumed effective throughput gain is 8×, and encoding and decoding add 2 ms of accelerator time per synchronization. Does compression shorten the synchronization, and by how much?

Math:

  1. Compressed communication time: 40 ms divided by the effective factor 8 = 5 ms.
  2. Total new time: 5 ms (comm) + 2 ms (overhead) = 7 ms.
  3. Effective speedup: 40 ms divided by 7 ms \(\approx\) 5.7×.

Systems insight: Compression is a trade of compute for bandwidth. With compression ratio \(\CompressionRatio\) and encoding cost \(\CodecOverheadTime\), it pays off only when \(\CodecOverheadTime< \CommunicationTime(\DeviceCount) \times (1 - 1/\CompressionRatio)\), that is, when encoding costs less than the communication time it removes. In this case, spending 2 ms to save 35 ms of network time is a clear win. On a high-speed NVLink network where \(\CommunicationTime(\DeviceCount)\) is only 1 ms, the same compression logic would cost more than it saves and slow down training. Always profile the network before adding compression.

The second test spans the full training run, because a compressor that passes the per-step test can still lose once slower convergence adds iterations. Comparing total wall-clock time to the same quality target on a slow and a fast network shows how a modest step-count penalty can reverse the verdict.

Suppose a training run requires 100,000 steps to reach its quality target without compression, with each step taking 500 ms (300 ms compute and 200 ms communication), for a total of 50,000 s. INT8 compression with error feedback of an FP32 payload reduces communication by 4× (from 200 ms to 50 ms per step), but suppose it raises the required steps by 10 percent, to 110,000. The new total is 110,000 steps at 350 ms each, or 38,500 s, a 23 percent improvement. Compression wins here because the per-step saving of 150 ms outweighs the additional steps.

The same model on a faster network, where communication takes only 30 ms per step, reverses the verdict. Compression cuts that to 7.5 ms, saving 22.5 ms per step, but still adds 10,000 steps at 307.5 ms each. The run now takes 33,825 s instead of 33,000 s, 2.5 percent longer. In this serial compute-plus-communication model, the break-even value of \(\CommunicationComputeRatio\) depends on the assumed compression ratio and extra steps, with no separate encoding cost included. A training schedule with overlap must instead compare the exposed time saved against encoding and convergence costs. Scheduling transfers during independent computation offers another way to reduce that exposed time.

Checkpoint 5.4: Gradient compression decisions

Verify your understanding of when and how to apply gradient compression:

Self-Check: Question

  1. A distributed training team applies Top-k sparsification with \(K_{\text{top}} = 0.001 \times d_{\text{grad}}\) (retaining 0.1% of elements) to FP32 gradients (\(d_{\text{grad}}\) elements at 4 bytes each). Transmitting the sparse gradient requires sending both FP32 values (4 bytes) and INT32 index locations (4 bytes) for each surviving element. What is the realized compression ratio compared to the dense FP32 gradient baseline?

    1. \(500\times\), because each kept element requires 8 bytes (value plus index), yielding an encoded payload of \(8 \times 0.001 d_{\text{grad}} = 0.008 d_{\text{grad}}\) bytes compared to the \(4 d_{\text{grad}}\) dense baseline.
    2. \(1000\times\), because index encoding overhead is completely eliminated by the network interface hardware.
    3. \(100\times\), because the error-feedback accumulator doubles the size of the transmitted index buffer.
    4. \(32\times\), because sparse tensors automatically conform to 1-bit quantization limits.

  2. Why is the Error Feedback mechanism (\(e_{t+1} = g_t + e_t - v_t\)) mathematically essential when applying aggressive lossy compression (such as 1-bit SGD or Top-k sparsification) in distributed training?

    1. It dynamically recompiles CUDA backward kernels to eliminate all register spills during gradient calculation.
    2. It converts AllReduce operations into asynchronous point-to-point transfers to bypass network switch buffers.
    3. It forces all model weights to remain strictly positive throughout optimization.
    4. It preserves deferred residuals, so average transmitted and original updates differ by \((e_1-e_{K+1})/K\), which vanishes if the residual grows sublinearly.

  3. How does 1-bit Adam achieve high compression ratios on optimizer state communication without suffering the severe convergence degradation of naive 1-bit SGD?

  4. True or False: Applying \(4\times\) gradient quantization will always decrease end-to-end training step time because reducing communication payload bytes is unconditionally beneficial regardless of whether the cluster is in a compute-bound or communication-bound regime.

  5. Describe the two phases of 1-bit Adam and the dependency that connects each compressed momentum message to the next residual-corrected update. Does residual storage have to wait for network transmission to finish?

See Answers →

5.10 Communication-Computation Overlap

Even with hierarchical routing and gradient compression, waiting until the entire backward pass finishes before launching AllReduce leaves every GPU idle across the full transfer window before the optimizer can update the weights. Communication-computation overlap hides that stall by running transfers concurrently with independent backward-pass computation, attacking the communication time that algorithm selection and compression leave exposed. How much of a transfer can be hidden depends on which parts of that transfer occupy the processor and which occupy only the network.

5.10.1 The LogP model of what overlap can hide

The α-β model estimates transfer duration without specifying how much independent computation can proceed during that transfer. Adding the entire estimate to compute time therefore overstates step time when overlap is possible. The LogP model (Culler et al. 1993) separates network transit from processor involvement with four parameters:

Culler, David, Richard Karp, David Patterson, Abhijit Sahay, Klaus Erik Schauser, Eunice Santos, Ramesh Subramonian, and Thorsten von Eicken. 1993. “LogP: Towards a Realistic Model of Parallel Computation.” ACM SIGPLAN Notices 28 (7): 1–12. https://doi.org/10.1145/173284.155333.
  • \(\LogpNetworkLatency\) (latency): The time for a message to traverse the network (similar to \(\MessageStartupLatency\)).
  • \(\SymmetricMessageOverhead\) (overhead): The processor time spent initiating or completing a transfer. During this work, the same execution resource cannot compute, making \(\SymmetricMessageOverhead\) the nonoverlappable cost. In a distributed training context, dispatch, device-kernel launch, memory registration, and runtime progress can all contribute. The value is an effective parameter of a particular software and hardware path, so it must be measured with the same collective, process group, and topology used by the workload.
  • \(\MessageInjectionGap\) (gap): The minimum spacing between consecutive sends or consecutive receives at a processor. It limits endpoint message rate but does not describe every source of link contention in a routed fabric.
  • \(\DeviceCount\) (rank count): The number of ranks in the communication group. The original model names this processor count with its final letter, P; this chapter writes it \(\DeviceCount\) to match its other collective cost formulas.

LogP distinguishes network latency (\(\LogpNetworkLatency\), which can be hidden) from processor overhead (\(\SymmetricMessageOverhead\), which cannot). Independent computation can overlap the network transit interval, while initiation and completion overhead remain exposed on the execution resource that performs them.

Napkin Math 5.8: Hiding communication behind computation
Problem: A training pipeline attempts to overlap gradient AllReduce with the next layer’s backward pass. The backward pass takes 500 μs. Represent the complete transfer by a toy budget with network time 100 μs, processor overhead \(\SymmetricMessageOverhead=\) 50 μs to initiate, and \(\SymmetricMessageOverhead=\) 50 μs to receive. Assume both overhead intervals occupy the same execution resource as backward computation, with independent network progress and no contention. Can the communication be hidden under these assumptions?

Math:

  1. Overlappable portion: Network latency \(\LogpNetworkLatency\) = 100 μs (data in flight while GPU computes).
  2. Non-overlappable portion: \(2\SymmetricMessageOverhead\) = 100 μs (GPU busy initiating/receiving).
  3. Compute available: 500 μs.
  4. Hidden: All 100 μs of \(\LogpNetworkLatency\) can overlap with compute.
  5. Exposed: The 100 μs of \(2\SymmetricMessageOverhead\) overhead cannot overlap.

Result: Effective time is 100 μs + max(500 μs, 100 μs) = 600 μs. The network latency is hidden, but the processor overhead remains exposed. Applied to every layer of a backward pass, the same split determines which gradient transfers a training step can hide.

Systems insight: The α-β model captures the total communication time. The LogP model reveals how much of it can be hidden. When designing pipelined training, optimize for low \(\SymmetricMessageOverhead\) (kernel bypass, GPUDirect) rather than high \(\LinkBandwidth\) alone.

The \(\MessageStartupLatency\)-\(\LinkBandwidth\) model estimates transfer duration from latency and bandwidth measurements. LogP adds the processor/network distinction needed to estimate exposed delay in pipelined execution. Choosing a communication schedule therefore requires both a transfer budget and the independent computation available to overlap it.

When messages are large and pipelined across the network, the LogGP model (Alexandrov et al. 1995) extends LogP with a gap-per-byte parameter \(\LongMessageGapPerByte\), modeling the streaming cost of long messages. These models describe multiprocessor communication with endpoint rate and capacity limits; they are not restricted to one flow on one link. Their compact parameters do not explicitly represent every shared path, oversubscribed cut, or routing hotspot. For congestion-sensitive workloads, particularly AllToAll for MoE, measurements on the target placement must test whether the model captures the active bottleneck.

Alexandrov, Albert D., Mihai F. Ionescu, Klaus E. Schauser, and Chris J. Scheiman. 1995. “LogGP: Incorporating Long Messages into the LogP Model—One Step Closer Towards a Realistic Model for Parallel Computation.” Proceedings of the Seventh Annual ACM Symposium on Parallel Algorithms and Architectures - SPAA ’95 30: 95–105. https://doi.org/10.1145/215399.215427.

↪ The LogGP long-message cost model and its gap parameters are developed in The LogGP model extension.

5.10.2 Layer-by-layer overlap

Gradient computation during the backward pass proceeds layer by layer, from the output layer back to the input layer. The gradient for layer \(\ell\) is available as soon as that layer’s backward pass completes, even while layers \(\ell-1, \ell-2, \ldots\) are still computing. That ordering lets the gradient for layer \(\ell\) start communicating immediately while the GPU computes the gradient for layer \(\ell-1\). Over a four-layer backward pass, the transfers for layers 4 through 2 run beneath later backward work, and only the transfer for layer 1, which becomes ready last, is left exposed before the optimizer step (figure 10).

In PyTorch’s DistributedDataParallel, this overlap is implemented through gradient hooks. Each parameter registers a hook that marks its gradient ready. Once all required gradients in a bucket are ready, the reducer can launch an asynchronous AllReduce, preserving the same collective order across ranks. Meanwhile, the backward pass continues computing gradients for earlier layers. A transfer is hidden only if it completes before its result is needed; queued communication can consume part of that window, and the last bucket’s transfer remains exposed (section 5.10.4).

The effectiveness of this overlap depends on the relative sizes of computation and communication at each layer. For transformer models, the largest gradient tensors belong to the attention and feed-forward weight matrices, which are also the most computationally expensive layers. That computation cannot hide the same layer’s gradient transfer, because the gradient is ready only afterward. It can hide a previously launched transfer; the ordering and cost of the remaining layers determine each gradient’s overlap window.

The overlap strategy interacts with algorithm choice from section 5.6 and section 5.7. An isolated-collective crossover need not predict the best training-step schedule, because the dependency schedule determines how much of each candidate’s transfer time is exposed. Overlap with the backward pass can reduce Ring’s exposed latency penalty, but the benefit must be measured with the actual gradient-ready times and runtime progress behavior. The optimizer step still cannot begin until every required reduction completes, so the transfers of the last layers processed, those closest to the input, stay exposed.

5.10.3 Bucket fusion

The layer-by-layer overlap described in section 5.10.2 assumes that each layer’s gradient is communicated as a single message. In reality, a transformer layer contains dozens of individual parameter tensors (query, key, value projection matrices, output projection, layer norm parameters, feed-forward weights, and biases). Launching a separate AllReduce for every individual parameter would create thousands of small-message collectives, each paying the full \(\CollectiveCallStartupLatency\) overhead. To avoid this, DDP uses bucket fusion to group parameters into buckets (often configured around tens of megabytes, with 25 MB a common PyTorch reference point) and launches one AllReduce per bucket. The bucket size represents a trade-off: larger buckets amortize \(\CollectiveCallStartupLatency\) overhead but delay the start of communication (because the bucket cannot be sent until all its parameters have computed gradients). Smaller buckets start communication earlier but pay more \(\CollectiveCallStartupLatency\) overhead.

The optimal bucket size depends on gradient-ready times, tensor sizes, and network characteristics. Smaller buckets can start communication sooner when waiting for more gradients would consume the overlap window. Larger buckets can amortize startup when repeated small transfers dominate. Neither choice follows from the model family alone; the useful measure is the resulting training-step time, including the exposed final transfer. PyTorch’s bucket_cap_mb parameter in DDP allows tuning this trade-off.

Bucket membership also determines how early communication can begin. A bucket containing one late gradient must wait even if its other gradients are ready. DDP can use reverse parameter registration order as an approximation to backward readiness and, depending on configuration, rebuild buckets from the observed backward order. Registration order need not match forward execution order, especially in models with branches or reused parameters. Grouping gradients that become ready together helps preserve the overlap window while maintaining a consistent collective sequence across ranks.

5.10.4 Overlap limits and exposed communication

Even well-tuned bucket fusion cannot hide all communication behind backward compute. Three structural limits (processor launch and completion overhead, pipeline boundary stalls at the start and end of backpropagation, and the HBM footprint of in-flight buffers) cap how much transfer time can disappear. First, the LogP model’s overhead parameter \(\SymmetricMessageOverhead\) represents processor time consumed by initiating or completing transfers; in the simplified LogP budget, initiation and completion consume \(2\SymmetricMessageOverhead\) per operation on the participating execution resource, so when backward compute intervals are short relative to that overhead, hiding network transit still leaves processor overhead exposed.

The pipeline also has boundary effects. During the first backward computation, no gradient from this pass is ready to communicate. Once the output-side gradient is ready, however, its communication can overlap with the remaining backward work. At the other end, the input-side gradient has no subsequent backward computation to hide its transfer. More layers create a longer overlap window, but depth alone does not guarantee high coverage: if communication takes longer than each compute interval, a backlog remains even in a deep model.

A third limitation arises from memory pressure. Buffers used by an in-flight transfer must remain valid while independent computation produces or consumes other tensors. Their overlapping lifetimes can increase peak GPU memory use, although views and buffer reuse can avoid separate copies. For FSDP workloads, prefetching parameters can keep multiple materialized groups resident at once, trading memory savings for communication overlap. The engineering challenge is to hide transfers within the available memory budget, and FSDP’s prefetch and resharding policies are the controls that set this trade-off.

Together, processor overhead, boundary stalls, and per-layer transfer time determine how much of a collective schedule can hide behind backward computation. Pricing a realistic 32-layer transformer backward pass shows how much communication remains exposed when each layer’s reduction outlasts its compute window.

Napkin Math 5.9: Overlap budget for a 7B transformer
Problem: A 32-layer transformer model (7B parameters) is trained on 64 GPUs. Assume the data-parallel reducer communicates FP32 gradients, about 875 MB per layer, in 100 MB buckets, four times the common 25 MB default. Each layer’s backward pass takes 15 ms. Assume the cluster achieves 3 ms per bucket under its contention and protocol overheads, so each layer’s gradients take 27 ms, and assume equal layers and the same charge for the final partial bucket. What is the backward-plus-communication time with and without overlap?

Without overlap (sequential):

\(\SequentialBackwardCommunicationTime= \BackwardPassTime+ \CommunicationTime(\DeviceCount)\) = 480 ms + 32 \(\times\) 27 ms = 1344 ms.

With overlap (pipelined):

Each layer’s AllReduce (27 ms) runs in parallel with the next layer’s backward pass (15 ms). Since 27 ms > 15 ms, there is 12 ms of exposed communication per layer that cannot be hidden.

Communication runs back to back from the moment the output layer’s gradients are ready. Each later reduction overruns its backward window by the exposed amount, so the backlog grows by that much per layer, and the last reduction adds its full duration after the backward pass ends:

\(\OverlappedBackwardCommunicationTime= \BackwardPassTime+ \LayerAllReduceTime+ (\LayerCount - 1) \times \LayerExposedCommunicationTime\) = 879 ms.

Systems insight: Overlap reduces backward-plus-communication time by 34.6 percent. The remaining exposed communication comes from the AllReduce being slower than the per-layer backward pass. To reduce the backlog, either lengthen the independent compute window (for example, with a larger batch) or reduce AllReduce time. The final gradient still has an exposed transfer tail, and any change must preserve the intended training workload and quality target. The overlap is most effective when per-layer backward compute exceeds the reduction time (\(\LayerBackwardTime > \LayerAllReduceTime\)).

The verdict turns on the assumed per-bucket charge. The hierarchical model of notebook 5.4 prices one uncontended 100 MB bucket on these eight nodes at about 0.85 ms, or 7.6 ms per layer, inside the 15 ms backward window, so at that rate every transfer except the last would hide. An overlap budget is therefore only as good as its achieved per-bucket time, which the calibration of section 5.11.2 measures rather than assumes.

An overlap budget joins the collective’s transfer cost to the model’s dependency schedule. Realizing that budget requires a runtime whose asynchronous progress and device-memory path preserve the independent work available between gradient production and consumption.

Self-Check: Question

  1. In a toy overlap budget, independent computation takes \(600\,\mu\text{s}\) and the complete network interval takes \(200\,\mu\text{s}\). Initiation and completion each cost \(50\,\mu\text{s}\) on the same execution resource as that computation. Assume independent network progress and no contention. What is the effective elapsed time, and which component remains exposed?

    1. Effective time is \(600\,\mu\text{s}\), and communication is 100% hidden because \(L_{\text{lat}} < T_{\text{compute}}\).
    2. Effective time is \(800\,\mu\text{s}\), because network latency and processor overhead must both be added directly to compute time.
    3. Effective time is \(750\,\mu\text{s}\), because the gap parameter \(g\) adds an irreducible stall to the compute pipeline.
    4. Effective time is \(700\,\mu\text{s}\) (\(2o + \max(T_{\text{compute}}, L_{\text{lat}})\)), where the \(200\,\mu\text{s}\) network latency is completely hidden behind the \(600\,\mu\text{s}\) compute, but the \(100\,\mu\text{s}\) of processor overhead (\(2o\)) remains exposed.

  2. True or False: In the stated toy LogP budget with independent work, network progress, and no contention, upgrading a network fabric to achieve lower physical wire latency (\(L_{\text{lat}}\)) will not reduce step time if the overlapped computation window \(T_{\text{compute}}\) already exceeds \(L_{\text{lat}}\) and the nonoverlappable processor overhead \(o\) remains unchanged.

  3. In PyTorch DistributedDataParallel (DDP), how does bucket fusion balance the trade-off between communication startup latency (\(\alpha\)) and the compute-communication overlap window?

    1. Grouping multiple parameter gradients into a bucket amortizes per-collective startup overhead, but the bucket must wait until all required gradients are ready, reducing the available overlap window.
    2. Bucket fusion compresses all gradient tensors into 8-bit integers before allocating them to ring communication buffers.
    3. Bucket fusion permanently disables all gradient hooks and forces all communication to occur after optimizer step completion.
    4. Bucket fusion ensures that all layers in the transformer model have mathematically identical parameter counts.

  4. In the illustrative 32-layer, 7B-parameter FP32 gradient budget, each equal layer has 875 MB of gradients. Charging nine 100 MB buckets at 3 ms each gives 27 ms of communication per layer, while backward compute takes 15 ms. Under a single serialized communication stream, calculate total backward-plus-communication time with and without overlap and explain the exposed tail.

  5. Explain the startup and drain boundaries of bucketed backpropagation that limit communication-computation overlap even when intermediate layers hide communication successfully.

  6. Why can one late gradient delay a DDP bucket whose other gradients are ready? Explain how parameter registration order and observed backward readiness can guide bucket membership without guaranteeing an optimal overlap schedule.

5.11 Communication Libraries and Runtimes

An overlap schedule only works if the communication backend can move the required tensors while independent computation proceeds. Production libraries realize the same algorithms and schedules, so the engineering decision is which one preserves the chapter’s model, topology, and overlap assumptions on the target hardware.

5.11.1 Backend selection

A collective runtime that bounces GPU tensors through host DRAM over PCIe or ignores NVLink and rail topology can cut effective reduction bandwidth by an order of magnitude even when it implements the right ring or tree schedule. Four properties therefore separate communication backends in practice: the device memory path, topology awareness, portability, and observability. NCCL leads on the first two for NVIDIA GPUs because it executes the collective algorithms of section 5.6 and section 5.7 directly on GPU-resident buffers (Jeaugey 2017; NVIDIA 2026). Its kernel fusion combines reduction arithmetic with data movement so intermediate results do not bounce back to HBM. GPUDirect RDMA lets the network card read and write GPU memory directly over PCIe without staging payloads through host DRAM. To keep multiple links busy at once, channel pipelining splits each collective across parallel communication channels, though realized throughput still depends on NIC placement, routing, and contention because extra channels cannot create port capacity. Documented environment variables then expose these choices for observability, with NCCL_ALGO and NCCL_PROTO inspecting or overriding the selected algorithm and protocol and NCCL_DEBUG reporting the active topology, channels, and schedule (NVIDIA 2026).

Message Passing Interface Forum. 2015. MPI: A Message-Passing Interface Standard, Version 3.1.

MPI matters when portability, CPU-side coordination, or HPC integration is the binding requirement (Message Passing Interface Forum 2015), and two of its features bear directly on overlap. Non-blocking collectives such as MPI_Iallreduce return a request handle that the application tests or waits on, a completion model different from NCCL’s ordering on CUDA streams. Persistent collectives, introduced in MPI 4.0, initialize an operation once and activate it at every step, amortizing setup for a training loop that repeats the same AllReduce shape. Either model hides a transfer only when independent work exists and the runtime makes progress. MPI’s one-sided communication (MPI_Put, MPI_Get, MPI_Accumulate) maps onto RDMA hardware and suits asynchronous designs in which one process writes another’s memory without its participation. CUDA-aware MPI implementations accept GPU pointers, but the selected transport decides whether data still passes through host memory, so that path must be benchmarked against NCCL.

Portability is the strength of Gloo, a PyTorch backend for CPU tensors, development, and testing whose TCP transport exercises collective semantics on one machine before a job reaches an accelerator cluster. Whether a faster shared-memory transport is active must be measured rather than assumed. Other accelerators have their own backends, AMD’s RCCL for the ROCm stack and Intel’s oneCCL, because the same algorithm has different effective \(\MessageStartupLatency\), \(\LinkBandwidth\), and topology constraints on each platform. PyTorch’s torch.distributed routes each call to the backend its process group names, so teams often mix them, with NCCL reducing gradients in a GPU group while a CPU group uses Gloo for barriers, scalar broadcasts, or sampler state. Applied to common scenarios, the four properties select a library, or a pairing of libraries, for each (table 13).

The table follows from the chapter’s cost model. A backend’s memory path and topology choices determine how it uses the fabric, and its diagnostic signals help identify whether \(\MessageStartupLatency\), \(\LinkBandwidth\), or processor overhead limits the job. The resulting performance must still be measured with the workload’s collective and placement.

5.11.2 Calibrating collective communication models

When an engineer runs nccl-tests on a new cluster and compares the reported AllReduce time against the naive point-to-point expression \(\MessageStartupLatency+\CollectivePayloadSize/\LinkBandwidth\), the achieved bandwidth appears to be roughly half of the link specification even on a healthy fabric. The bandwidth-latency trade-off (principle 5) provides accurate first-order predictions only when it models the same collective that the benchmark executes: for a ring AllReduce over \(\DeviceCount\) ranks and an \(\CollectivePayloadSize\)-byte tensor, the first-order cost is \(2(\DeviceCount-1)\MessageStartupLatency+ 2(\DeviceCount-1)\CollectivePayloadSize/(\DeviceCount\LinkBandwidth)\), whereas a tree, a hierarchical schedule, and a switch-assisted reduction each carry their own step counts and traffic factors. NCCL also selects protocols, channels, and algorithms according to message size and detected topology, making those runtime choices part of the experimental condition (Jeaugey 2017; NVIDIA 2026).

NVIDIA. 2026. NCCL User Guide.

A calibrated collective model therefore records the operation, reduction datatype, rank count, tensor size, algorithm and protocol, topology, rank placement, and achieved bus bandwidth. It then fits effective \(\MessageStartupLatency\) and \(\LinkBandwidth\) to measurements from that same configuration, as The \MessageStartupLatency-\LinkBandwidth performance model does for one measured NCCL AllReduce. Comparing predicted and observed time under this contract reveals software overhead or topology loss without mistaking a collective’s algorithmic traffic factor for library inefficiency. The resulting parameters apply only to the measured regime; changing the collective, process group, topology, or library version requires recalibration. A calibrated model selects a collective schedule and sizes its overlap window. Attributing an observed training-step regression to kernels, memory, communication, or contention is a broader measurement task, which the program goodput analysis of Program Goodput develops.

Communication diagnosis begins by comparing the workload against a matched bare-collective baseline and recording the selected algorithm, protocol, rank placement, and achieved bandwidth. A healthy baseline with a slow training step points to invocation granularity, contention, stragglers, or insufficient overlap rather than the fabric alone. Profiler traces and the attribution workflow separate those causes; the output needed here is the calibrated \(\MessageStartupLatency\), \(\LinkBandwidth\), and overlap window that select a collective schedule.

The backend must satisfy both the collective contract and the measured execution budget. A fast isolated collective is insufficient when its placement, invocation order, or progress behavior leaves the training step waiting.

Self-Check: Question

    3. NCCL eliminates all network synchronization barriers by approximating gradient additions as stochastic random walks.
    4. NCCL replaces InfiniBand hardware with standard TCP/IP operating system sockets.

  2. According to the chapter library selection guide, which backend mapping is recommended?

    1. NCCL for macOS development, NCCL for CPU preprocessing, and Gloo for H100 training.

  3. When debugging a multi-node distributed training job that runs at 50% of expected throughput, what diagnostic information does running nccl-tests provide compared to profiling the PyTorch training loop directly?
